196. BRIDGET DALTON was indicted for stealing, on the 7th of November, 1 shift, value 2 s., the goods of Mary Ann Ladbury.
(The prosecutor did not appear.)
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18381126-197]
197. FRANCIS ASSIOTTI was indicted for stealing, on the 4th of June, 2 sheets, value 2 s., the goods of James Sands.
MARGARET SANDS . I am the wife of James Sands, of Bloomsbury, a lodging-house keeper. I let the prisoner a room—he lived in it a month and then left—I missed the sheets off the bed, and every thing from the room that he could make sixpence of—these are my sheets—( looking at them)—they were pawned at Mr. Newberry's, who produced them before the prisoner, and the prisoner said he had pawned them—he was not authorised by me to pawn them.
WILLIAM HAYDON ( police-constable F 54.) I took the prisoner and have the sheets, which the pawnbroker gave up.
Prisoner's Defence. It was from poverty I did it, but with intent of returning them.
GUILTY .— Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18381126-198]
198. FREDERICK PEARSE was indicted for stealing, on the 15th of November, 1 clock, value 5 l. 5 s., the goods of Henry Hudson.
GEORGE WILLIS . On the afternoon of the 15th of November, about four o'clock, the prisoner came up-stairs in my master's coffee-house, and ordered a cup of tea, and toast, and bread and butter—the clock was then safe, but it did not go-in half-an-hour it was gone—I have not seen it since—I saw the prisoner go—he had his coat thrown over his arm, and something bulky under it—he had asked me before for the paper, and when he came down he said, "Never mind the paper now, I shall be back in an hour," and when I went up stairs there was some dirt on the table, and the works of the clock were all taken away-only the bare case hung up—there was no one else there—there were two ladies and a gentleman there half-an-hour before—the clock was there then.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Is the clock-case here? A. Yes, this is it.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18381126-199]
199. FREDERICK PEARSE was again indicted for stealing, on the 8th of November, 1 clock, value 5 l., the goods of James William Wetter.
JANE PLANK . On the 8th of November, about eight o'clock in the morning, the prisoner came to my master's, James William Wetter's coffee-house
in Coventry-street, for breakfast—we had a clock there—I had seen it safe between eight and nine o'clock in the morning—we missed it half an hour after he was gone-no one else was there—the case and all was gone.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Is it a large room? Yes, divided into boxes—there was no one there during the time the prisoner was there—he was there half-an-hour-people go in and out—I will not say, on my oath, that no one else went in—I saw no one go up—it was a large dial-a Mackintosh-cape would cover it.
MARIA WARD . I am cook at the house. I saw a young man, answering the prisoner's description—I did not see his face—he hurried out of the passage with a Mackintosh-cape over his shoulder.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18381126-200]
200. FREDERICK PEARCE was again indicted for stealing, on the 27th of October, 1 clock, value 2 l., the goods of William Robert Webber.
WILLIAM ROBERT WEBBER . I am landlord of the Lamb public-house, in Turnstile. On Saturday afternoon, the 27th of October, between four and five o'clock, the prisoner and another person came to my parlour—I left them there alone—atthe expiration of ten minutes I went up, and they were gone, also the clock and case, which had been safe when I left them.
Cross-examined. Q. Are you sure there were any works in the case? A. Yes—I had seen it open the day previous—the dock went—I am the sole proprietor of the house—the prisoner and his friend were not dining, only taking a glass of grog—my house is well frequented—they and three more were all who were there that afternoon.
ARTHUR CAMPION . I was at the public-house that day—the prisoner and some other young man were there—I remained till a quarter to five o'clock—I left the prisoner, and the young roan, and the landlord, only in the room—the clock was safe then—I returned in about twenty minutes—the clock was then gone.
Cross-examined. Q. How long were you there? A. From ten minutes past four to a quarter to five o'clock—one gentleman came in, and left the room before I did—Mr. Webber must make a mistake when he said there were three persons-what I speak is correct—I am sure there was but one more person came in.
MR. WEBBER re-examined. Q. Did you mean three? A. The prisoner and his companion, and two more—there were one or two, and then there came in two, I think—they went out and left the prisoner and his companion there.
Cross-examined. Q. What time did Mr. Campion, come in? A. I cannot say to five minutes—I will say about four o'clock—there were two or three persons—I cannot say which—I will swear there were not four—I remember Campion going out, and no one came in after he went away.
(Alexander M'Intire, a messenger;—Dearman, of Harley-street, a hackneyman; and Francis Lownds; gave the prisoner a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 21.— Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18381126-201]
201. JOHN SPARKS was indicted for stealing, on the 29th of November, 1 sheep-skin, value 7 s., the goods of Daniel Fielding.
ROBERT NEATE . I am in the service of Daniel Fielding, of Rosoman-street, Clerkenwell. I saw the sheep-skin safe on the 29th of November,
and between five and six o'clock I saw the prisoner come and take it away—he walked off and dropped it—I took it up, and gave him in charge.
Prisoner. I had not the skin. Witness. Yes, you had, and I took you—I was set to watch the skins.
GUILTY . Aged 42.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18381126-202]
202. JOHN WILLIAMS was indicted for stealing, on the 29th of November, 1 handkerchief, value 2 s., the goods of John Wright, from his person.
JOHN WRIGHT . I live in St. Paul's Churchyard. About a quarter before six o'clock on the evening of the 29th of November, I was in Watling-street—I missed my handkerchief—on turning I saw the prisoner and another man walking towards Friday-street—they turned up there—I followed them, and told a policeman—I overtook the prisoner near the top of Friday-street, in Cheapside, and he was then blowing his nose with my handkerchief—I asked if he had not been in Watling-street with another man—he denied it—the policeman then came up and took him—he then had my handkerchief in his pocket—this is it—it has my initials on it.
THOMAS CARTER ( City police-constable No. 72.) I took the prisoner—I asked him what he had got in his pocket—he said, "Nothing at all"—I found the handkerchief in his pocket.
Prisoner. I picked it up in Friday-street.
GUILTY . Aged 21.— Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18381126-203]
203. LOUISA DALES was indicted for stealing, on the 27th of November, 1 gown, value 5 s., the goods of Emily Hand.
EMILY HAND . I am single, and live in New Compton-street, Soho. I work in the same room with the prisoner—on the 27th of November I left my gown hanging there, and the prisoner was in bed—when I returned in the evening she was there, but my gown was gone—I asked if she had parted with my dress—she said, she had not—I told her if she had, to deliver me the ticket, or tell me where she had parted with it, and I would forgive her—she did not tell me—this is my dress—( looking at it.)
Prisoner. Q. Did you not tell me to take your dress? A. No—I wanted it particularly in the evening.
EDWARD WILLIAM HUMBERSTONE . I am a pawnbroker. This gown was pawned by the prisoner on the 27th of November.
Prisoner. She was distressed—I got her into work.
EMILY HAND . No, she did not.
GUILTY . Aged 34.— Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18381126-204]
204. JAMES GARRETT was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of January, 83lbs. weight of sugar, value 2 l. 5 s., the goods of John Dale.
MR. BALLANTINE conducted the Prosecution.
JAMES DALE . I am assistant to my father, John Dale; he is a master carman, and lives in Stoney-lane, Tooley-street. On the 10th of January I gave Dennis two hogsheads of sugar—he was a carman of ours—I had known the prisoner before—he had been in our employ six or seven months—Dennis was to take one hogshead to Mr. Sayers, and another to Mr. Davies, of Cullum-street—he was gone longer than he ought to have
been—I went in search of him, and found the cart standing in Fenchurch-street, not far from Mr. Davies's—he had then been gone about two hours, and it was only five minutes' walk from Mr. Sayers' to Mr. Davies's-before I got to the cart I saw the prisoner get out of it, jump off the shaft, and run away—I have not the least doubt he is the man—I had an opportunity of seeing him enough to know him—Dennis was by the side of his horse, and when he saw me he ran off—I have not seen him since—I took the horse and cart to Mr. Davies's, and then I got into the cart, and found a bag of sugar alongside of the hogshead—that sugar had been taken from the drawing-hole—the tin was off it—there were 83lbs. weight of sugar in the bag, the same as that in the hogshead; and there was an iron hoop in the cart, with which they had worked the sugar out, and the sugar was on the hoop—I took the hogshead back to Mr. Savers', and it was weighed—it weighed 14cwt. 7lbs., and in the bag was 83lbs. weight, which would make up the weight that was originally in the cask.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. What time was this in the day? A. From twelve to two o'clock—I had known the prisoner a long time—I do not know that he lived in the very street that I do—I do not know whether the sugar-house is in Lambeth-street—I saw the prisoner get out of the cart very plainly—I knew him very well—I dare say I saw his face for two or three minutes—I saw the cart standing still—I did not hurry myself to go up to it—there is no doubt but the prisoner saw me for two or three minutes.
EDWARD DAVIES . I live in Cullum-street On the 10th of January I ordered a hogshead of sugar of Mr. Sayers—it was to be sent to me, but it did not arrive so soon as it should—it ought to have weighed 14cwt. 3qrs. 6lbs.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you know the prisoner? A. No, I never saw him till he was at Lambeth-street.
JOHN ISAAC POHL . I am house-clerk to Mr. Davies, a sugar-refiner in Lambeth-street. On the 10th of January I saw a cask of sugar weighed at his warehouse—it weighed 14cwt. 3qrs. 6lbs.—I have the book in which I put it down—I saw it loaded into Mr. Dale's cart—I did not know where it was going.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you weigh it? A. No, the warehouseman did—I saw the weights in the scale—the man puts the weights into the scale, and then calls out the weight—but I always look at the weights myself—I do not move them before I look at them—I will undertake to say I saw those weights—it was the weight of the first hogshead, No. 251.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. That No. 25I was the weight you have mentioned? A. Yes—I sent a note of the weight with the cart.
EDWARD DAVIES re-examined. The hogshead out of which the sugar was taken was No. 251, and the weight I ought to have had was 14cwt 3qrs. 6lbs.
JAMES LEE . I am an officer of Lambeth-street I took the prisoner on the 7th of November, which was the first opportunity I had of taking him.
Cross-examined. Q. What do you mean, that he had absconded? A. I do not know—I was not aware he was wanted till a fortnight before, and then he was in the House of Correction—when I brought him away, he said he was there charged with stealing sugar from the London Docks, but he had not stolen it, though he had been there for two months.
JAMES DALE re-examined. We gave information the next day to the Thames Police—we were not able to get him into custody.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you go to look after him yourself? A. No, the officers of the Thames Police did—they stated they had been to his house for him.
JAMES LEE . I know the Magistrate's signature. I saw the prisoner examined, and heard what he said-what is down on this examination it what he told me, and what he said at the office—( read)—"The prison" says I was not in the cart, and know nothing of the transaction at all."
(Two witnesses gave the prisoner a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 18.— Transported for Seven Years.
Fifth Jury, before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.

205. FRANCIS DURBAN was indicted for stealings on the 25th of November, 10 planes, value 5 s.; 2 saws, value 1 s. 6 d.; 4 hinges, value 1 s.; 6 chisels, value 1 s.; 1 stock, value 2 s. 6 d.; 30 bits, value 1 s. 6 d.; 2 squares, value 1 s. 6 d.; 1 gimlet, value 2 d.; and 1 brush, value 3 d.; the goods of George Maberley.
( The prisoner being deaf and dumb, had the evidence explained to him by signs.)
CHARLES MILLS ( police-constable T 72.) I was on duty at New Brentford, on the evening of the 25th of November, at half-past seven o'clock—I met the prisoner in the street—his pockets appeared bulky—I took him to the station-house, and found in his pockets the tools stated—the sergeant wrote down questions on the slate to the prisoner, and he said he had the tools from his uncle at Hanwell—I went there, and found it was false.
GEORGE MABERLY . I live at New Brentford. These are my tools—I lost them from my workshop yesterday week—I know the prisoner perfectly well—I had employed him the same day—I never gave him these tools.
Prisoner (through an Interpreter.) I meant to put them back again—I will never do it any more.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 29.— Confined Eight Days
[trialAccount t18381126-206]
206. RICHARD VARLEY was indicted for stealing, on the 15th of October, 4 salt-cellars, value 14 l. 10 s.; 2 mustard-pots, value 4 l. 10.; 4 muffineers, value 6 l. 10 s.; 1 wine-funnel, value 2 l. 8 s.; 1 bell, value 2 l. 12 s.; 2 cups, value 6 l. 8 s.; 3 tea-pots, value 33 l.; 1 coffee-pot, value 14 l. 17 s.; 1 sugar-basin, value 8 l.; 1 jug, value 5 l. 14 s.; and 1 watch, value 20 l.; the goods of Henry John Lias and another, his masters.
MR. DOANE conducted the prosecution.
HENRY JOHN LIAS . I live at No. 8, Finsbury-square. The prisoner was in the employ of myself and partner, for three months, as clerk and town traveller, at 80 l. a year—it was his duty, among other things, to carry out goods on approbation—on Friday, the 12th of October, I sent him out with the articles here enumerated—he was to show them to a person in the City, and bring them back—he did so, and also on the following day—on Monday, the 15th, he took them again, and asked me if he might take the gold watch in the drawer to show, saying, "I
think you said you would take 20 l. for it?"—I said yes—I have the book in which is the list of the things enumerated, with the exception of the gold watch, and they were entered on the 12th—I saw no more of the prisoner till the 6th of November, when I found him in custody—I have never got my gold watch, and there is one child's cup missing—the value of the whole property was about 118 l.
Cross-examined by MR. JONES. Q. Did you receive a good character with the prisoner? A. I did, from Mr. Savory—I allowed him no commission in any way—I have never made any remuneration or present to him after he had sold a lot of goods for us, and never promised him any—on one occasion he took a journey into the country, and was absent seven or eight days—he charged his expenses, and I paid him—I did not allow him any thing as a present to himself—I never promised him a commission or bonus on what he sold-no conversation ever took plate between us on the subject—he has been trusted with property to a larger amount than this—I should say 500 l. or 600 l.—I think not to the amount of 800 l. or 900 l.—he was authorised to sell all the articles with the exception of one tea-pot, which was a pattern one—he was authorised to sell the rest at the price fixed in the indictment, nothing below that—I swear he had no discretion—I left nothing to his judgment—the amount of the whole was 118 l.—he was not authorised to sell at less than that—he would not have been allowed to sell at five per cent off that—I had taken the per centage off—his directions were to sell them for ready money—the party he went to, Mr. Savory, was a ready-money customer—he was not restricted from selling them to any other person, if he sold them at the price fixed—he has not on other occasions exercised his own judgment, as to what price he was to sell at—I gave him these on the 12th, and the last time I saw him was on the 15th—he locked them up in the iron safe, on my premises, at night, and took them out in the morning—these were quite new goods, and fashionable articles—they were what is called, "shaped"—some of them were very richly chased-a great part of the work was done by the maker, and that is very expensive-if it had been left as it came from the maker, it would not be plain, it would be shaped—the shaping is the ornament—this is a shaped tea-pot—( showing one)—it is chased-part of it was done by the maker, and part by the chaser-if it were shaped only, it would be much cheaper than if it was chased—this is not the cottage tea-pot—the prisoner had no authority to sell this tea-pot at any price—this is the one that was merely for show—he was to sell the others by weight and fashion—he would have sold them by the whole set—I have a copy of the list which he took with him—I did not make it at the time—I made it from this book, which contains the list of the articles, but not the prices—the prices he had with him—I made out this list after his apprehension, not after I had indicted him, nor after I had charged him at the police-office—it was after I caused him to be taken into custody—I was not straitened in circumstances for ready money at the time I gave the prisoner these articles—I did not tell him when I gave him them, that they and other stock must be turned into money, as I did not know how to turn, or how to go on for cash—he might have sold these things for 118 l. with the exception of this tea-pot—he had no general authority to sell to whom he chose, without he sold for ready money—these articles would be worth about 12 s. an ounce to a trader-8 s. or 10 s. would not be a fair price—there are articles here
that are 16 s. an ounce—I do not know that there are silversmiths who would sell them for much less than that.
MR. DOANE. Q. You have stated a price below which he was not to sell these articles? A. Yes—he was not authorised to sell any at the rate of 8 s. 6 d. an ounce—the price at which he was to sell them would average about 12 s. an ounce—I did not authorise him to walk off with the gold watch—this is the property he had—( looking at it.)
SAMUEL LAWRENCE . I live in Agar-street, Strand. On Sunday, the 4th of November, I received this letter, in consequence of which I went to the Russell coffee-house, in Windmill-street—I found the prisoner there—I ultimately bought this property of him, for 70 l. 10 s. 9 d., at 8 s. 3 d. an ounce—I paid him some on account—I sold it the next day to Mr. Solomon, for 81 l. 4 s.
Cross-examined. Q. Was 8 s. 3 d. a fair price? A. It was.
HENRY SOLOMONS . This is the property that I got from Mr. Lawrence.
COURT. Q. When did you buy it? A. On the 5th of November, at twelve o'clock, at 9 s. 6 d. an ounce—I paid 81 l. 4 s. for it—on the Tuesday I left for Brighton, for a week.
Cross-examined. Q. You bought them from a trader? A. Yes—I have known him for twenty years—he produced them on the counter, and I was looking over them for some time.
JAMES BRANNAN ( police-constable G 20.) I went and got this property from Mr. Solomons.
Cross-examined. Q. Did yon not hear the prisoner say he had been in liquor and lost the money? A. I did not.
HENRY JOHN LIAS re-examined. I believe this letter to be the prisoner's writing—I have seen him write—this is his writing in the book.
Cross-examined. Q. Have you been comparing this with other writing of his? A. Not at all—I think it is in his ordinary hand-writing—I have not the least doubt about it.
( Letter read)—"Sunday morning. Mr. Walford, jeweller, &c., from Liverpool, was recommended to Mr. Lawrence, as being likely to purchase a quantity of new plate, which Mr. W. has brought up with him from Liverpool, for immediate disposal. It consists of tea plate in sets, and mugs, mustard-pots, &c.; nearly all of which are gilt inside, and quite new, modern, and about 200 oz. In explaining that it must be sold immediately, and at a great sacrifice, no doubt it is necessary Mr. W. should explain, that having a very pressing engagement to pay a sum of money on Tuesday morning in Liverpool, which will, after Christmas, yield Mr. W. an enormous profit, he is induced to make the temporary sacrifice, for one that will be so much more to his advantage. Mr. W. begs to say he must be in Liverpool on Tuesday morning, with at least 50 l. in cash; the remainder as might be agreed upon, Mr. W. would not object to a bill. Mr. W. only came in just now from Liverpool; and if Mr. L. will call on him at the Russell coffee-house, 26, Great Windmill-street, he can see the goods, and Mr. W. will wait at home this morning to see him."
(Thomas Hughes Headland, No. 13, Great Sutton-street, Clerkenwell, a silversmith; and Charles Hayward, a silversmith, in the Minories; gave the prisoner a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 31.— Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18381126-207]
207. ANN DAVIS and MARY ANN NOWLAN were indicted for stealing, on the 29th of November, 1 pair of boots, value 7 s. 6 d., the goods of Matthew Hesses.
BENJAMIN CROYDON . I am in the service of Matthew Hesses, a shoemaker, in Ratcliffe-highway. On the 29th of November the prisoners came to his shop, in company, about six o'clock in the evening—Davis asked if a pair of shoes that she had ordered about a fortnight previously were ready—(Nowlan was with her when she came before)—I said they were—I asked her to sit down, and gave her the shoes to try if they would fit her—she declined trying them on, and said she thought they would suit, and gave me a sovereign to pay for them—she then asked me to give her a pair of strings—I said I had not got any, unless I took them from another pair—while I was doing that I missed a pair of boots, which had been down right before me, hanging against the door-Nowlan was nearest to them—(I had missed two pairs the time before, when they came to order the shoes)—I called Barker to mind the shop, while I got a policeman—the prisoners remained in the shop—I got a policeman, and asked him to search them—he turned round, and found secreted in a corner the very pair of boots I had missed—I cannot say what had occurred while I was at the door—these are my master's boots—I had given them change for a sovereign.
ISABELLA BARKER . I was at the further end of the shop—I saw Nowlan stoop down in the corner where the boots were afterwards found—I did not see the boots in her possession—I did not know they were lost till I saw the policeman take them up—there were other boots where these were.
CHARLES PATTEN ( police-constable K 206.) I was sent for—I looked round, and saw where the boots had been hanging, and the string they hung by had been cut.
Davis's Defence. When Barker came in, this man said, "Stop a bit, I want to speak to you"—as it rained, I came in and stood there—the policeman came in, and he and the policeman talked two minutes—the policeman looked about, and the shopman put the boots on the counter for the policeman—they talked a bit—he then said, "I suspect these two of stealing the boots"—I said, "I have no one to wear the boots—I have had no husband for two years"—I opened my cloak—I had nothing to cut them with—I have six small children.
Recommended to mercy by the Jury.
DAVIS— GUILTY . Aged 39.— Confined Nine Days.
NOWLAN— GUILTY . Aged 19.— Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18381126-208]
208. BENJAMIN JACOBS was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of November, at St Botolph-without—Aldgate, 1 neck-chain, value 3 l. 10 s.; 2 rings, value 3 l.; and 2 pairs of ear-rings, value 3 l. 10 s.; the goods, of Lydia Emanuel, in her dwelling-house.
LYDIA EMANUEL . I am a widow, and keep a clothes-shop, in Harrow-alley, Houndsditch, in the parish of St. Botolph-without—Aldgate; it is my dwelling-house. On Saturday, the 10th of November, before I went out, I went into my bed-room, opened my drawers, and locked them all safe—I left this property in the drawers—I went out between six and seven o'clock in the evening, leaving a female servant at home—I was not out half an hour before my grandchild came and called me—I found my house full of people, and the room in great confusion, these things were gone, and others packed up ready to take away.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. How came you to go to your drawers before you went out? A. To take my spectacles out—these two rings were in my beaufet, and the ear-rings also—they were all in one little drawer.
MARTHA DODD . I am the prosecutrix's servant. I recollect her going out-her bed-room is up one pair of stairs—when she went out I bolted the street-door after her—I cannot tell how any person could have got into the house, or whether they got in before or after she went out—I was sitting—atneedlework in the kitchen, which is level with the ground, and heard a noise up stairs—the stairs come into the kitchen—I thought it was the cat, and I called her, but instead of her coming down stairs, she came to me from under a chair—I then sat still, and heard another noise—I took no notice, and I soon after saw a light pass by the crack of the bed-room door—I thought my mistress had left the light up stairs—I went to go op, and, in going up a few stairs, a man opened the bed-room door, with a candle in his hand—I was so frightened that I stopped a few minutes, and the man too—I never shall forget the look he gave me—I got out as soon as I could, and gave the alarm, and the man got out of the front bedroom window—I hallooed out in the street, and persons came to know what was the matter—I told them, and ran up stairs, and saw my mistress's things, the plate and wearing apparel, all down on the ground, and packed up, the window-curtains torn down, and eight or nine locks broken—the prisoner was taken that night, and I swore to him that night—I am certain he is the man I saw—he could not have got in after my mistress went out, because the door was bolted.
Cross-examined. Q. How could you see the light under your mistress's door? A. The bed-room door was right opposite me—I can see from the kitchen up to the bed-room door—there are but a few stairs—there is no door to the kitchen—I could look right up to the bed-room door-a great many persons came into the house, but they were pretty well all my mistress's family, her children and grandchildren—the prisoner looked at me so hard I thought he would have pierced me through—I was very much flurried indeed-as he jumped out of the window the tail of his coat caught my comb, and broke two teeth out.
ANDREW DACEY ( police-constable H 94.) I received information, and took the prisoner at ten o'clock that night—his dress was described to me, and I knew him previously to this—I took Dodd to the station-house—she swore to him positively, and said, if it was her last words on her dying bed, that was the man.
Cross-examined. Q. Where did you take him? A. In Wentworth-street, about five minutes' walk from the prosecutrix's—there are slop-shops and old clothes-shops in Harrow-alley.
GUILTY . † Aged 19.— Transported for Ten Years.
[trialAccount t18381126-209]
209. GEORGE DEANE was indicted for a misdemeanour.
JAMES REVELL . I am shopman to John Sherborn and others, oil and colourmen, at No. 321, Oxford-street. On the 16th of October the prisoner came to the shop, and wished to know the price of shellac—I told him—he said he would take 121bs. weight, and 21bs. weight of white gum—I asked if they were to go down to Mr. Frinneby—he said, "Yes"—I should not have given him the goods unless they were to go to Mr. Frinmeby—I
knew the prisoner as his servant—we deal largely with him, and for that reason gave him credit.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Were the goods made up? A. Yes, and I offered them to him—he said he must have a bill, or it would not be right.
FREDERICK RICHARD FRINNEBY . I am a brush-maker. The prisoner had been my servant—he was not so on the 16th of October—I did not send him for these things—he did not go by my orders.
WILLIAM KING ( police-constable G 120.) I took the prisoner, and the shopman asked him whether he was prepared to pay it, or could he pay it.
Cross-examined. Q. Did he ask him if he was the man that came? A. Yes—I said I would have no compromising—he then said he would give him in charge.
GUILTY . Aged 18. Confined Three Months.
OLD COURT.— Tuesday, December 4 th, 1838.
Second Jury, before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
[trialAccount t18381126-210]
210. THOMAS FREEMAN was indicted for a misdemeanor.
MR. DOANI conducted the Prosecution.
CHARLOTTE MATTHEWS . I am single, and live in Perry-street, Somers town. On the 3rd of November the prisoner came to me—I was not acquainted with him, but I had seen him once before, twelve months ago, when he had brought me a letter from a friend—he did not bring any letter on this occasion, but he said since I had seen him last he had been a voyage to Sierra Leone, and had seen my friend on board the Dolphin, to which he belonged—he produced these papers to me, and said there was 22 l. due to him on them, which he was to receive at Somerset-house on the 15th of November, and asked me if I could advance him 1 l. till the Monday, when he would repay me—my sister gave him 15 s. by my direction—I cannot read myself, but the papers were read over to me by a friend in the prisoner's presence—I believed them to be genuine, and advanced him the money in consequence of his representing that they were so, and that he belonged to the ship he named, otherwise I should not have dope it—he left the papers with me as security—he dined with me on the Sunday after, and on Monday I went with him to Somerset-house—he went into the gates, but whether he went into any office I cannot say—when he returned he said it was the 5th of November, which was a public holiday, and the money could not be paid till the Monday following—I went again with him next day—he went into the same place, and said the clerks were engaged, or gone to lunch, and he could be paid in an hour—these are the papers he gave me—I have never had any of the money back again. ( The papers being read, one purported to be a certificate from the London Dock Registry, of good character, to Thomas Freeman, on a voyage to Sierra Leone, in the Edward Mills, Captain Harry, and entitling him to 15 l. 5 s. wages; and the other a document from the Admiralty, entitling Thomas Freeman, able-seaman, to 22 l. 5 s. prize money, for service on board her Majesty's ship Dolphin, Edward Blankley, Commander.)
THOMAS KING WALCOT . I am a clerk in the Admiralty. I have the
muster-books of the Dolphin —I find on them the name of Thomas Freeman—he was paid the residue of wages due to him, on the 13th of November, 1837, amounting to 6 l. 8 s. 3 d.—that was all that was due to him on account of wages—I know nothing of any prize-money being due to him—he was on board from the 17th of September, 1836, to the 18th of September, 1837—he was in fact on board only till August the 22nd, 1837, but was paid up to the 18th of September, by a regulation of the service—the Dolphin was not paid off—he was discharged—he afterwards entered as a volunteer on board the Electra, on the 31st of January, 1838, but ran away from her on the 14th of September, at Portsmouth, and having done so, he forfeits all wages or prize-money that might have been due to him—one of the papers produced relates to the merchant-service, and I know nothing of it—it would not entitle him to any money from us—the other purports to be a certificate from the Admiralty, but it is a fabrication altogether, such a certificate would never be given there, it would not entitle him to receive any wages.
Prisoner. I received some other wages from the Rolla. Witness. I know nothing about that—the Rolla is not mentioned in these papers.
JOHN MATSON . I am a clerk in the Prize-office, Somerset-house. There does not appear to be any prize-money due to the prisoner-all that he was entitled to was paid—this document, dated from the Admiralty, is a forgery—Captain Blankley, whose name is stated as Captain of the Dolphin, was not the captain of that vessel at that time.
HENRY DEWDNEY . I am Superintendent of the Seaman's Registry in the Commercial-road. This document appears to be dated from my office—there are no such parties as stated in this, in any way connected with our office—it is a fabrication-our papers are printed—this is written—it it a fabrication from beginning to end, the name of the master, ship, office, and every thing.
ISAAC MARCH ( police-constable E 41.) The prisoner was given into my custody on the 12th of November, in Tottenham-court-road, by Mr. Matthews, for obtaining money under false pretences—he said he would pay the money back again.
( The prisoner put in a written defence, stating that he had no intention of defrauding the prosecutrix, that he had been twenty years in the navy, and had a wife and two children.)
GUILTY . Aged 40.— Confined One Year.
(There were two other indictments against the prisoner for similar offences.)
[trialAccount t18381126-211]
211. EDWARD BUTTON was indicted for a misdemeanor.
MR. DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM FLOOD . I am a porter. On Thursday afternoon, the 15th of November, the prisoner who lives at Mr. Campbell's, at the Crown, in Butcher-hall-lane, told me to go for twopenny worth of cantharides—I got it at Mr. Watkins's, and gave it to him—he gave me 2 d. for my trouble.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Where did you give it to him? A. In the shop—I always knew him to bear the character of a decent, wellconducted person.
MARY ANN WELLS . I was living at Mr. Campbell's—the prisoner was waiter there. On Friday morning, the 16th of November, I breakfasted
at half-past nine o'clock—my master and mistress, and the prisoner and his wife had had their breakfast together soon after eight o'clock-just before I had mine, I saw the prisoner go up stairs as I was coming down—he had an opportunity of getting to the coffee before I had—I afterwards drank a small portion of it, and it made me ill—I saw Mr. Hadlow the doctor.
Cross-examined. Q. You only drank very little? A. About half a cup—my sister took a little—I was about my business next day—I had had no quarrel with the prisoner—we were on friendly terms—his wife was living in the house at the time as servant, for about a fortnight—she went away the evening this happened—about half-a-pint of coffee was made for the second breakfast.
ELIZABETH WELLS . I am sister of last witness. I went up stairs to Mrs. Campbell, when I found out the disagreeable taste in the coffee, and told her of it, but she had drank of it then—she only had about two table spoonfuls—she was in a delicate state of health before.
JAMES CAMPBELL . I keep the Crown. On the morning of the 16th I was with my wife when she took the coffee—it made her ill, and I had the prisoner taken into custody—he said he had given it out of a lark.
HENRY HADLOW . I am a surgeon in Jury-street, Aldgate. I was called in to attend Mrs. Campbell, on the morning of the 16th of November—I saw the coffee, and there was cantharides in it sufficient to produce an injurious effect to any one taking it—Mrs. Campbell was very seriously affected by it for twenty hours, and is an invalid still.
Cross-examined. Q. She was an invalid before? A. She was—I could not tell what quantity there was-twopenny worth mixed in half-a-pint of coffee would produce most serious consequences, if all taken by one person-Mary Ann Wells and Mrs. Campbell were both ill, and it was necessary to apply powerful remedies—Mrs. Campbell labours under a stomachic disease, not connected at all with the occasion of my being called in to attend her in this case, but it was aggravated by this—she is now under my care.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 23.—Recommended to mercy.— Confined Tweleve Months.
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18381126-212]
212. JOHN HUTTON STALWORTHY was indicted for embezzlement.
ISAAC BRYANT . I was shopman to Mr. Henry Moyce, King-street, Convent-garden; but I have left—the prisoner was also a shopman in his service. On Saturday evening, the 27th of October, after lighting the gas, I went to the desk and counted the money on it—there were three half-crowns, a fourpenny-penny, and 3 s. or 4 s. in copper—I left it on the top of the desk—the prisoner was in the shop at the time-a woman came in, and was served by the prisoner—I saw her pay him 1 s. 6 d. and 3 d. in coppers—the prisoner went to the desk and put 9 d. there—the shilling was put somewhere else—I went to the desk directly after, and there was no shilling there—I asked the prisoner how much money he had taken of that lady—he immediately said "1 s. 9 d."—I said, "Where is the shilling? there is no shilling there"—he said, "Perhaps it is under the coppers, perhaps it has fallen down behind the desk"—he was going round to see himself, but thinking perhaps he would put the shilling there, I pulled him back a little, and there was no shilling there when I went round-when
my master returned I told him what had happened-no one but the prisoner had been to the desk, from the time I had left the half-crowns and coppers there, nor from the time the prisoner went there till my master came.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. How long had the prisoner been in your master's employment? A. A fortnight—he had very likely been gaining his master's good will very fast—I know he had—I am not in the employment now—I was discharged—my master did not direct me to watch the prisoner—I told Johnson, on Saturday morning, of my intention of doing so—I had not heard that any money was lost—I had reason to watch him, for on the Wednesday or Thursday before, I saw him drop some money from his hand, and look very much confused—that was the reason I watched him—the 1 s. 9 d. is entered in the book, but very likely that was put down afterwards—he did enter it—he asked me to go with him to the desk, and look behind, to see if the shilling was there, but I thought he would put it there—I went directly after, by myself-Johnson was serving a customer in the shop at the time—I had had no words with the prisoner—I was by the side of him when he put the 9 d. on the desk—I went directly after and saw no shilling.
Q. If there had been one, you had the opportunity of removing it? A. Yes, but I did not—I left a month after this, because I was ill—I was out one night-two gentlemen assaulted me, and bad to pay 5 l. each for it—I slept Saturday night in the station-house—I was with my sister and another young lady, in George-street, Strand, and they wanted to take the inside of the pavement—I was eight or nine months in the prosecutor's employ—I was discharged about a fortnight ago, on a Tuesday night—the assault was on the Saturday—I was ill on Saturday, and did not go back till Tuesday—I went to the theatre on Monday night, instead of returning to my master's—it was very foolish of me—I was out Saturday night, Sunday night, and Monday night, without giving him notice—I told my master I had been ill—I was discharged for staying away.
THOMAS JOHNSON . I am in the employ of Mr. Moyce. On Saturday evening, the 27th of October, I counted the money on the desk, after Bryant—there was three half-crowns, a sixpence, a fourpence, and some coppers—these told me to count it—the prisoner was serving a customer while I did so—he took 1 s. 9 d. of the customer, and went and put some money on the desk—these went and counted it—I went afterwards, and found there was one shilling short of what the prisoner had taken—I did not remove any thing—I am quite sure there was no shilling there when I went—these might, if so disposed, have taken the shilling off before I went.
Cross-examined. Q. Were you not very much surprised at Bryant asking you to go and count the money? A. Yes; he told me on Saturday morning that he suspected him—I had a slight suspicion myself, as be several times dropped money, and said it was very slippery—he said so in Bryant's presence—these was discharged for being out two or three nights—these asked the prisoner what he had taken from the customer, and he said, "1 s. 9 d. "—I did not look to see whether he entered it in the book—he ought to have done so, but it is sometimes not done when we are very busy—my master had not desired me to watch the prisoner—he never intimated any suspicion about him, or complained of money being deficient.
JAMES WESTON ( police-constable F 62.) I apprehended the prisoner, on
the 27th of October, at his master's, about a quarter before nine o'clock in the evening—I searched him, and found on him a five-shilling-piece, two shillings, and threepence in halfpence.
Cross-examined. Q. I believe he declared he was perfectly innocent? A. He did, and said it was a shocking thing that he should be taken up on such a charge, when he could have money at any time from his uncle.
MR. PHILLIPS called
HENRY MOYCE . The prisoner was in my employ for a fortnight I was very much pleased with his conduct up to the present time—I gave him into custody from information received from Bryant—I had no alternative—I have a leaf here which I saw taken out of my book—it is dated, "October 27th"—here is 1 s. 9 d. entered as received by the person entering it—I cannot tell whether it is the prisoner's writing, but I believe it is—there are eight or ten entries after it—I dismissed Bryant for being out on Saturday, Sunday, and Monday nights—I said to him when he came back, "What do you mean by this?"—he said, "I was ill, and could not come"—I said, "Don't tell me so, for I have proof that you were at the theatre on Monday night"—I had received a message from a young woman on Monday night, about eight o'clock, that he was very ill in bed, and could not come, and that very night he was at the theatre—he wished to stay with me, but I said on no account would I have him, on account of his misconduct—he had 25 l. a-year—the prisoner had 30 l.—these did not know what the prisoner had.
NOT GUILTY .

216. JOSEPH ALEXANDER TREVETHEN was indicted for a misdemeanor.
MESSRS. CLARKSON and BALLANTINE conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM RAFFEHEEPS . I am clerk to Mr. Ramsbotham. About the 10th of September the prisoner called at my master's counting-house, (I did not know him before) and wished to purchase some low-priced sherries, saying he had been referred to us by Mr. Lindsay—I told him Mr. Ramsbotham was not at home, but he had two or three parcels that were just landed in the London and St. Katherine Docks, and I appointed him to meet Mr. Ramsbotham at the Docks—Mr. Ramsbotham could not go, and
I went and met the prisoner at the Docks, either the same day, or the next—I showed him a parcel containing four butts, eight hogsheads, and four quarter casks—he inquired the price—I referred him to Mr. Ramsbotham—he said the quantity was immaterial—he talked very largely, and, I think, said he was a man of property—he made an appointment to see Mr. Ramsbotham.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Did you hear what passed between Mr. Ramsbotham and him? A. No; I heard him say the deed in question was worth four or five hundred pounds—I do not know which—it was not three thousand pounds.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Is Mr. Lindsay a person known to Mr. Ramsbotham? A. Yes, and known at the Docks.
CHARLES WHEEN RAMSBOTHAM . I carry on business in Dunstan-court, Mincing-lane. On the 11th of September last, I went to St. Katharine's Docks in consequence of an appointment made by my clerk—I saw the prisoner there—he was a stranger to me—he said he had been told that I had some wines on the quay, which would suit his purpose, and he wished me to make a bargain with him for them—he wished to offer me a price for them—he had already seen them with my clerk in the morning—he looked at them again, and said they would suit him—he talked about other wines, saying he wanted to purchase very largely of other qualities—atlast it was decided that he would have these four butts, eight hogsheads, and four quarter-casks, which formed one parcel, and he would call on me next day about it—he told me he was a man of property, but was not much known in the market—that he had lately had cash left him, and he intended to pay cash for every thing he bought—he called on me next morning, between ten and eleven o'clock—the price was then named, and fixed at 17 l. per butt, which would be altogether 170 l.—I asked him for a reference—he told me he always wished to satisfy parties he did business with—that he intended to name a party he had been travelling for, but he had had a dispute with the house, and consequently would not wish to refer me there, but he stated he had lately come into possession of property, which he had received as heir to his father—I told him he had said he intended it to be a cash transaction—he said he intended to pay cash, but that that day he was short, and he would give me the title-deeds of his property, of which he had lately come into possession, as security for the payment of the money in the course of a few days, and he produced to me this deed—( looking at one)—he particularly pointed out to me this part, that it was to be held by Thomas Trevethen, his father, his heirs and assigns, as absolute property, and stated that the property was waste land at the time the document was written, but it had been built on, and was then worth 500 l., and in accounting for the indorsement on it, he said that a short time before it had been in the hands of some party for an advance of 200 l., but as this would only be for a few days, it was not worth while indorsing it again—he deposited the deed with me as security for the payment—he then asked me for the transfers, as he could not get the wines, or transfer them to any other party before I had transferred them to him—I ultimately gave him the wine warrants, in consequence of the representations he made with respect to this instrument—I had refused to do so before—I parted with them believing what he said was true—I have never received any cash since—I had occasion to leave town on the Saturday after I had parted with the warrants, the 15th, and Mr. Ramsay, a friend of mine, undertook the management
of my business in my absence—I mentioned to him the transaction between the prisoner and myself, under what circumstances I had agreed with him, and what had been deposited with me—I never authorised Mr. Ramsay to take bills of exchange in payment from the prisoner for the wine—it was arranged in the first instance that I was to be paid immediately-subsequently, in the after part of the day, when the prisoner had got possession of the warrant, he said he thought he probably should not be able to pay cash so soon, but part of the property that would come to him on the 29th of September would be in cash—I remonstrated against this delay, but as I was obliged to leave town immediately, I had no alternative—some of the wine is gone from the Docks.
Cross-examined. Q. Have you not had some of the warrants back again? A. No, not any, I believe the officer has three warrants for hogsheads, but they form a very small part of the whole, which was twenty hogsheads—some bills of exchange were sent under an envelope, during my absence, to my counting-house, but they have never been received as payment—some of them are on a Mr. Bragg of Bristol—Mr. Bragg has not given me up any warrants—Mr. Bragg waited on me some time ago, and made an offer to give up part of the wines he had, on my cancelling the bills against him, which he stated had been obtained by Mr. Trevethen under false pretences, but I have since discovered that Mr. Bragg has got an advance on those wines, and they are no longer in his power to deliver to me—I understand Mr. Bragg is a man of no responsibility at all—he told me he could not pay 6 d. towards the bills—he did not tell me his wife was a person of property—he said she had no property—the prisoner told me that Mr. Bragg's wife was a person, of property—that he had got a bond on her property—I believe he said so when we first talked about the parties to whom the wine was going—when I found that the money was not forthcoming, I called on the prisoner to know what he had done with the wines, and he gave a kind of history of what he had done or was going to do with them—he mentioned Mr. Bragg as a person to whom he had parted with some—he did not name him as a person whose bills he would give me—decidedly not—there was no talk about bills at all—he mentioned Mr. Bragg as a highly respectable tradesman of Bristol-now I happened to know Bristol very well—I referred to the name of Bragg, and asked who he was—he said he had been for a long time with a house he named, which an intimate friend of mine had lately established—that he was going to be one of his greatest friends and correspondents, and that he had got security from Mr. Bragg to the extent of 4 or 500 l. in a bond, I understood him to say, of some property of his wife's or family's—the bills on Mr. Bragg were one of 81 l. and one of 70 l.—the wine he had was worth 51 l.—the prisoner was apprehended on the 29th of October—I delivered him the warrant on the 11th of September.
Q. Was there not a further shipment of wines agreed for between you and the prisoner? A. Not agreed for—he wished me to make an agreement for them, and of course if he had paid me the cash for these I should with pleasure have gone on in further business with him—this deed was not given as a deposit for any further shipments-nothing of the kind was mentioned—one of the bills that were sent was on a Mr. Phillips for 19 l.—Mr. Phillips has never called on me and offered me the money on my taking off the discount—I never heard any thing of the kind—I have seen Mr. Phillips—the bill is payable at Smith, Payne and
Smith—the object of his calling on me was to tell me it was at Mr. Trevethen's request that he accepted the bill, and that he made it payable at Smith's, at Mr. Trevethen's request, because it would look better—his object in calling was not to pay the bill—he never offered to pay it, on my making a reduction.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Did you at any time from the beginning to the conclusion, agree or consent to take bills of exchange for your goods? A. Certainly not, of any kind whatever—nor did I authorise any body to do it for me.
ANDREW RAMSAY . I am a wine-merchant, and live in George-street, Minories. About the 13th or 14th of September, I was requested by Mr. Ramsbotham to take charge of his business during his absence for a few weeks—I did so, and saw the prisoner at Mr. Ramsbotham's counting-house—I told him that in undertaking the management of Mr. Ramsbotham's business during his absence, and he telling me he had transactions with him for four butts of wine then on the quay, I wished to know his account of it—he mentioned something about giving bills, and some were afterwards sent to the counting-house—I had received no authority from the prosecutor to take bills in payment—I never received them in payment, only as a deposit.
Cross-examined. Q. A bill is always a deposit—I suppose you took them, and if they were paid it was all right, if not, yon held the man responsible who had the goods? A. The bills had four months to run—I could not have taken them in that sense—the prisoner was to pay the amount by the 9th of September, but in the mean time he deposited the bills as security—the prisoner told me that he had given Mr. Ramsbothan security for 500 l.—the prisoner brought one bill to the Dock in the first instance—that was on Bragg and Co. of Bristol—I took that bill, but not in payment—I told him at the time I took it, that I wanted the money—I cannot tell on what day it was—it was between the 14th and 29th of September—he again repeated that on the 29th of September, he would bring the money—he had the warrants in his possession at the time—I saw him about the Docks everyday, and was every day urging him for the money-very likely eight days might elapse between his giving me the first bill and my receiving the others—he gave me another on Mr. Bragg, which I discovered was an altered bill—I recommended him to draw it afresh for his own safety—he said he had no change to purchase a stamp, and Mr. Ramsbotham's clerk gave him the money for it—he afterwards brought the bill properly drawn and left it with me—I have the bills here—they are drawn at four months.
Q. Is not a bill at four months in the wine trade reckoned cash? A. Certainly not—nor at three months-not even bankers' bills, if they have only one month to run—I gave the bills to Mr. Ramsbotham's clerk to lock up till his return, and I was urging in the mean time payment of the money from the prisoner.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Did you tell the prisoner you would not consent to receive them as payment for the wines? A. Undoubtedly, in the most pointed manner—I explained to him that I merely received them as a deposit for the money, he assuring me that the 29th of September would be the latest period of his being in ample funds to pay the money.
HENRY LE GRICE . I am an attorney at Bury St. Edmunds. I produce the proceedings in a commission of bankruptcy against the prisoner,
on the 8th of September, 1835—the usual parts of them have been enrolled, the fiat, the adjudication, and the appointment of assignees—I have retained possession of them—I know the prisoner to be the person against whom the fiat was issued—( looking at the deed given to the prosecutor)—I know the property comprehended in this deed—there was no statement in the'balance-sheet of the prisoner of his having any such property—the assignees afterwards discovered that he had a reversionary interest, on the death of his mother, in the property, and they put it up for sale—the prisoner knew of that—he called on me after I had been down to Bury and seen the property, in 1837—I then questioned him about it, and he said he had forgotten it—he gave that as a reason for not having put it in his balance-sheet, and he gave the same answer to the commissioners—I was present when he was examined at the Insolvent Debtors' Court—he was discharged under the Insolvent Act—the date of his examination before the Commissioners was the 24th of March, 1836—he afterwards called on me, previous to the sale, on the 23rd of May, 1837, and knew it was to be sold—it was put up with some copyhold property, and fetched about 75 l. the whole—there were some buildings on it, I believe, partly stables—( looking at a bill)—this is one of the bills of sale—the property formed part of lot 3, and was sold for 75 l. to Mr. John Trevethen, the prisoner's brother.
Cross-examined. Q. Do not you know it was bought by him for the prisoner? A. I do not know it—the conveyances were made out to Mr. John Trevethen—we should not have had them made out to a man who was a bankrupt, and had not received his certificate—I have known the prisoner ten or twelve years, living at Bury St. Edmunds—he left very shortly after he took the benefit of the Act—I believed him respectable up to the time of his bankruptcy.
JAMES SMITH ( police-constable H 113.) On the 30th of October I received the prisoner in custody from the prosecutor—on our way to the station-house he asked me with what he was charged—I told him, for obtaining wine by false pretences from Mr. Rarasbotham—he said he wished to have the business settled, and he had thrown himself in the way on purpose—he gave up some papers, among which were these three warrants, for a hogshead of wine each.
MR. RAMSBOTHAM re-examined. This is the deed the prisoner showed me, and this was the part he particularly pointed out—( reads)—"to be vested, &c, for the benefit of Thomas Trevethen, his heirs and assigns, as his private and absolute property"—it was believing what he stated about that which induced me to part with the warrants—he never told me it had been sold to his brother, or that his brother held it—he never mentioned his brother—he said he was heir to his father—he did not mention his mother at all.
MR. PRENDERGAST called
JOHN TREVETHEN . I am the prisoner's brother, and have been a builder at Bury St. Edmunds for twenty-seven years. I bought the property in question by the prisoner's request, I believe for 155 l. but I do not know—I never saw the documents—they are principally copyhold, except two small pieces of field—I bought all the property he had under his father's will, at his request—I bought some lots, and Mr. Wayman bought some—I only bought his interest in it—I know there is a cottage on it, worth 4 l. or 5 l. a-year, and there may be about half an acre of land, but I forget, having been away so many years—I refused to buy it when he first asked me—the property is not
conveyed yet—I bought it by auction—I told him at the time I would not let him have it till he was in a condition to receive it—Mr. Le Grice has the cottage—I have not paid the 155 l.—I paid a deposit—I should say the two included are worth 400 l. or 500 l., but I know nothing about it—it was sold as it was, under lease to Murty, but I never saw the deed—it has not been offered for my signature.
COURT. Q. Had your brother any communication with you, to authorise the deposit of this document, when he was in treaty for the wine? A. No, he had not—he solicited me to purchase on three occasions—I believe the property was advertised for sale, and not sold, at first—I saw that two pieces of freehold land were left out, which belonged to him, and that made me look after it—I informed Mr. Le Grice's clerk, and they were inserted afterwards—when he was able to receive the property he was to have it.
GUILTY . Aged.— Confined Six Months.
NEW COURT.— Tuesday, December 4 th, 1888.
Fifth Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18381126-217]
217. ANN NORMAN, alias Streeton, alias Hathaway ; MARY ANN GRAFTON , CHARLES FOSTER , THOMAS GUY, alias John Thompson ; and JAMES HOBBS , were indicted for conspiracy.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution.
ROBERT NATHANIEL BURTON . I am a corn-factor, and have an office in Bush-lane. I have a cottage at Finchley, which I was desirous of letting—the prisoner Norman called on me at my office, and represented herself by the name of Ann Streeton—she said she had seen my cottage, and wished to take it—we very shortly agreed about the terms, which were 40 l. a-year—she said she had lived at the New-forest, Southampton, for some considerable time, and that she would give me a reference to her late landlord's brother, whom she had a house of there—that he was the best possible reference, as he knew how she had paid her rent, and she referred me to Mr. Hobbs, (the prisoner,) New Manor-street, Chelsea—we came to an agreement, and I wrote it out, stating the terms for three years, for which time she was to take the house—I wanted her to take it for five years, but she said it was impossible, she would not be tied to a house so long—she called for the agreement, and said she must have possession immediately, the following day or two, as her furniture was on its way from Southampton, and she must have the house to go to at once, which I agreed to—on the second day, when she called to sign the agreement, she asked if I had a wine-merchant, and I named my wine-merchants, Messrs. Gill & Co. of Mark-lane—this was on Monday or Tuesday, I think on the Friday following I received information, and went down to my cottage on the Sunday morning—I found there the prisoners Norman, Grafton, and Foster—I asked Norman how she could account for her conduct—she said it was all over now, and there was no help for it—I found them nearly destitute—there was no furniture in the house—they had used the kitchen drawers for tables and chairs, and there was a dirty old bed on the floor, which was all I could see—they were taken on the Wednesday, by the policeman, to Edge ware, and I think from there to prison.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. You found them in a destitute state? A. Yes, in a room with a great number of old beef bones, and other things.
JAMES PARKER . I am brought from White Cross-street prison. I did live at No. 41, Ely-place—I called at the request of Mr. Burton, on the prisoner Hobbs, at New Manor-street, Chelsea—(I cannot recollect when it was)—I found Hobbs at a beer-shop—I told him, that Mrs. Streeton, of the New Forest, Southampton, had given a reference to him as to her respectability—he said he did not like to give characters in general, but the was a very respectable woman—I asked him if she had rented a house of his brother for seven or eight years—he said she had, and had always paid her rent regularly—I asked him what establishment she kept—he said, a phaeton, a pair of ponies, and I am not sure whether he said three or four servants—I told him she was about taking a house of Mr. Burton, at 40 l. a-year rent—he said she had rented houses of double that rent, and he should have no objection to let her one, only his own were not in a sufficient state of forwardness—he told me I should find her a close-fisted woman, but I should not have to call a second time for the rent.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Did you make any memorandum of the conversation? A. I did not—I afterwards inquired, and found that Mr. Hobbs passed himself off as a builder, but he was not known as such—I inquired of Sergeant Tedman, but did not make inquiries in the neighbourhood—I am quite sure that Mr. Hobbs did not say that Mrs. Streeton had told him she kept a phaeton—he did not say that he had seen it—I am not in Whitecross-street for debt, but through a mistake in a legal process—I have made application to a Judge to get out, and shall be out in a few days.
GOODMAN. ( Looking at an agreement)—This is my name, as a witness to this agreement—I saw Mrs. Norman sign it, in the name of Streeton, on the day it bears date.
( The agreement was here read for letting the house for three years certain,
and was dated 27th October, 1838.)
WILLIAM SMALLPIECE WHITTINGTON . I am clerk to Messrs. Gill, wine-merchants, of Mark-lane. The prisoner Norman called at my employers' counting-house on the 29th of October—she said she had taken a cottage of Mr. Burton, at Finchley, that he had named us as his wine-merchants, and she wanted some wine—I asked what wine she required, and she gave an order for three dozen of sherry, three dozen of port, and two gallons of brandy; and here is a subsequent order, in her writing, on the 1st of November, for one dozen of madeira, and two gallons of ram—the amount of the whole was 24 l. 3 s. 6 d.—she said I was to put down the prices as low as possible, as she always paid cash for the goods—the goods were sent by Drewett, the carrier—I went down to the house on a Monday, I think, the 5th or 6th of November—I found the house in custody of an officer, but I could not find the bottles or wine, or any part of the spirits-from information I afterwards received, I went to a house in Bridge-water-square, where I found some wine, and two gallons of rum in a jar—I could identify the jar of spirits as one we had forwarded to Finchley, and our cellarman could identify the wine—the bottles had been sealed when they left our counting-house, but the seals had been cut off close to the bottles.
COURT. Q. What house did you go to in Bridgewater-square? A. No. 15—the landlord came for me, and I went with him.
CHARLES DREWETT . I am the Finchley carrier. On the 31st of October I received two hampers from Mr. Gill, the wine-merchant—I delivered them at Rose Cottage, in Ballard's-lane, Finchley—I received a note from the servant at the house, which was the prisoner Grafton—I took the note to the wine-merchant, and left it there, on the 1st of November, and on the 2nd I took down from there two stone bottles in a basket, and delivered them at the same house.
CECILIA DREW . I keep a grocer and chandler's shop at Finchley. On the 27th of October I sent my young man to Rose Cottage for orders, and I afterwards sent some goods there—on the Thursday following I went to the house—the amount of my bill at that time was between 6 l. and 1 l.—I saw the servant Grafton first—I asked to speak to Mrs. Streeton—the servant told me she was engaged, and I was to go up in an hour afterwards—I went then, and saw Mrs. Streeton—I told her I had a large bill to pay, and I should be glad if she could pay me my bill, or part of the money—she said if I would send the bill on Monday she would pay me on Tuesday—that she had taken the house of Mr. Burton for three years, and she had given him a reference to the landlord of the house where she had lived for the last eight years—I bid her good afternoon, and we had no more conversation—there were some orders came to me on that Thursday, but I only sent part of them; and on the Saturday there were orders, but I did not send them—on the Monday I went with the bill—I saw the servant (Grafton) at the window—she took the bill of me—Mrs. Streeton also came to the window—she told me I need not wait, she should pay the bill, but should not pay it then—this is a copy of my bill—( looking at it)—it amounts to 8 l. 11 s.—I went to the house with the policeman the same afternoon—there were some stone jars and white jars of mine there—Mrs. Streeton said what there was left she would give up to me—some plums, and currants, and soap, and lump sugar, of mine, were brought down to Finchley, and shown to me by a young man.
COURT. Q. Who was it you saw these things with? A. Henry Stuckey.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Before you called at Mrs. Streeton's, had you delivered all these goods, except about 1 l. worth? A. Yes—I called for orders at the house, they did not make application to me—the goods I got back was about 1 l. worth—Mrs. Streeton did not tell me that she had given a reference to Mr. Hobbs, but to some place that Mr. Burton was satisfied with, and he had let her the house—I went by what she said.
HENRY STUCKEY . I am shopman to Mrs. Drew. On the 27th of October I received an order from the prisoner, Mrs. Streeton, for candles, soap, sugar, tea, and other things—I delivered them at the house, to the servant, Grafton—on the 28th, I took some more goods, which I delivered to Grafton—on the 30th, I delivered some more things—they consisted of blacking, plates, a dish, vinegar, nails, and other things—on the 31st, I delivered 1/4 cwt. of sugar, some butter, ginger, bacon, honey, and other things; and on the 1st of November, some scouring-paper, jars, and other things—I delivered them all to Grafton—I had very little conversation with her—I asked her where she came from—she said, "From Southampton"—I asked who her mistress dealt with there—she said, "Mr. Rymer, a cheesemonger, of East-street, Southampton"—that the family
had been in the habit of dealing with him for eight years past—she said they expected the remaining part of the family in a few days.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. I am afraid a great deal passed between you and this young woman? A. No, Sir—I do not make a practice of talking with all the servant-girls in the neighbourhood—I took a walk in the garden with Grafton, but that was at the time I was stationed in the house with the policeman, to take care of the property.
EVAN EVANS . I am a greengrocer, and live at Finchley—I deal in coals and greens. An order was left at my house, when I was not at home, for a sack of coals, to be taken to Rose Cottage—I took the coals to the cottage—I saw Grafton and Foster—I had the coals in two sacks—I shot one down in the little passage, and the other I left in the sack, in the scullery, according to their request—I received an order for grapes, apples, greens, and such things, from Grafton and Foster—they were acting as servants in the house, to the best of my knowledge—I delivered goods to the amount of 3 l. 15 s. 8 d.-both Grafton and Foster said I was to take my bill on the Monday, and I should be paid on the Tuesday following, at nine o'clock—I went upon the Sunday afternoon, like the rest of the sufferers, the tradespeople of Finchley—I cannot say how many there were—I believe I heard Foster say, it served us right for not going on the Friday night.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Who was the man you heard say this? A. I believe it was the boy Foster—I overheard it, and I believe it was him—I did not see him then—I had seen him a very little time before—I was listening, with my ear close to the door, the same as all the rest were, when it was said—I am sure I heard-it, and I believe, by the voice, it was Foster—I did not see him at the time-Norman, Grafton, and Foster, were in the house at the time—they had locked themselves in—I went up to see the last of them, like all the rest—I did not go to have them taken up.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. You had seen Foster shortly before you heard this? A. Yes—I and my brother tradesmen were outside the house—I had seen Foster at the back of the house—I could see into a small room which they were in at that time—I saw Grafton and Foster, and Mrs. Streeton came into the room—she said, "You shall have your money, whatever becomes of the rest, as you are a poor man, with a large family"—there was no other man or boy in the room, from the time I saw Foster there, till I heard them talking—I knew his voice, by his coming to my house for goods.
WILLIAM JAMES WRIGHT . I am a butcher, and live at Finchley. I did not know Norman till she was taken, but Grafton used to come to me, and order beef-steaks, mutton-chops, roasting-beef, and other things for her mistress, Mrs. Streeton—my bill came to 1 l. 15 s. 11 d.—on the Sunday after the goods had been delivered, I saw Bond, who is since dead, in the act of loading a cart at the back-door of the prisoner's cottage.
THOMAS RAKE . I am a livery stable-keeper, and live in the Curtain-road. I know the prisoner Guy—I always heard of his name being Thompson—he lived at Highgate—I do not know what he is by business, he never told me—he always had a green bag in his hand, whenever I saw him—I let him a chaise, about three or four months ago—I cannot say when was the last time—it is about three months ago—I never put it down, when persons pay as they go on—he said he was going to Edmonton and
Enfield—I believe he never mentioned Finchley to me—I do not recollect that ever he did—it was on a Sunday he had it last—I was not bound in recognizance to appear before the Grand Jury—I never set my hand to any paper—I did not abstain purposely from going before the Grand Jury—I did not know any thing about it—I did not go before the Grand Jury till I was sent for.
Q. On the last time, on the Sunday when he hired a chaise of you, what was it for? A. It was a cart he hired last to go to Highgate—he said he was going to take his family out—he did not say any thing about his children, nor about Finchley, I swear that—he hired the cart between eleven and twelve o'clock, to take his family up from Highgate, not to fetch his children from Finchley—that I swear solemnly.
COURT. Q. Were you sworn before the Magistrate? A. Yes, and what I said was put down in writing—I did not swear he was a tailor—I said I thought he was a tailor, as he had always a green bag with him.
Q. Did you swear before the Magistrate that it was between ten and eleven o'clock on Sunday morning, and that he went to fetch his children from Finchley? A. I do not know the time—he did not say "his children," he said his "family from Finchley"—he did not on that occasion pay me ready money, he did before—he now owes me 12 s. 6 d.—he hired the chaise in the name of "Thompson," and I thought that was always his name—I booked the 12 d. 6 d. to Thompson.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDSRGAST. Q. You took no particular notice of what be said? A. No—he had hired a chaise before, and paid me for that, and in consequence of that I let him have the horse and cart—I thought his name was Thompson—that is all I know—I cannot swear that he told me his name was Thompson—I did not write any name down, and I cannot say what it was—I thought it was that.
Q. You do not mean that you put down the name of Thompson? A. Yes, I put that name down—I did not take any address of him—I only put down the name of Thompson—that was the name I knew him by—I had only seen him once before—I do not know what was in the green bag.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Did he answer to the name of Thompson, both times? A. I did not ask him his name.
JOSEPH HIGGS . I am a horse-patrol. In consequence of information I received, I went on Sunday, the 4th of November, to Rose-cottage, Ballard's-lane, Finchley, about five minutes before six o'clock in the evening—when I got about 200 yards beyond the cottage, I saw a cart—the prisoners Guy and Mrs. Streeton were in it—there was on the cart the name of "Thomas Guy, East-street, Walworth," chalked on a piece of leather, and on the other side of the leather was printed, "Thomas Rake, 138, Curtain-road"—the chalked side was outwards—I spoke to Guy, and asked him whose cart it was—he said it was his, and he was hired to bring it down to Rose-cottage to take the things away which he had in the cart—I asked him his name, and he refused to tell me—I found in the cart about 1 cwt. of coals, some meat, and a box, some candles, some flannel, some tin-dishes, sugar, tea, a dripping-pan, a baking-dish, two chairs, and part of a pig's head—these things were wrapped in a kind of horse-rug in a pan, which covered over the coals and the things that were in the cart—the prisoner Norman did not say any thing at that time—I took them back to the King of Prussia public-house—I then pulled the leather off the cart, and saw Rake's name on the other side of it—I put the horse and cart up
and locked up the property—Mrs. Streeton said if I doubted that she was the person that lived in Rose Cottage, she would take me and show me, and the said the things were her property—(when I saw the things in the cart they were coming from the cottage)—I went to the cottage with her—she knocked at the door—the female servant came down, let her in, and she shut the door—on the next day I took Streeton, and Grafton, and Foster into custody there, and searched the house—there was a mattrass, three pillows, two wooden chairs, and an old table; a quantity of crockery packed up in a box, and coal-sacks with different names on them; some plates and dishes, two casks which had had beer in them; some candles, sugar, cheese, soap, and a few small articles of grocery, which Mrs. Streeton was giving up to Mrs. Drew, when I went in—I asked her what she was doing—she said she was giving them up, and she was quite willing to give up what was in the cart—I was desired by the Magistrate, to watch at Clerkenwell New Prison after the prisoners were committed—I saw a woman speak to Mrs. Streeton, and in consequence of what I observed pass, I followed that woman to No. 15, Bridgewater-square—the woman went into that house, and I watched there till the next morning—I then sent for Mr. Gill, the wine-merchant, and he procured Herdsfield, the City officer.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Did you take Hobbs? A. Yes, at Great Manor-street—I took him before the Magistrate, and brought him back—I charged him 80 s.—I did not tell him if he did not pay I would have his goods—his brother paid it.
THOMAS HERDSFIELD . I am a City officer. I was sent for to No. 15, Bridgewater-square, by Mr. Gill, on the 18th of November—I found there ten bottles of wine, and two gallon stone bottles—I believe there was brandy in one of them—Mr. Gill identified the stone bottles immediately—I gave up the wine and the stone bottles to Mr. Whittington—I found a quantity of grocery things which were identified by Drew—I delivered them up to a person for her—I found some ale and beer which was identified by Mr. Thompson, and the body of a chaise in the front parlour, and the wheels, springs, and shafts, in the yard.
ROBERT TUBBS , Esq. I am a Magistrate of Middlesex. I have a cottage at Harlesdon Green and five acres of land attached to it—I know the prisoner Ann Norman—she came to me in that name, early in the month of August—she treated with me for the possession of that cottage as a tenant—I have the memorandum which I made with her—I let the house to her at ninety odd pounds a year, for three years—the memorandum was signed by her in my presence—the cottage was occupied by her from the Saturday till the Tuesday week—I then received a letter inclosing the key, by the parcel-delivery company—the key opened the cottage—I had delivered it to Mrs. Norman-a few days after I received the letter I went to the cottage—there was no furniture there—the acres of land were let to her with the cottage—she described herself as a widow lady of property, who had been living some years at Bath, that she had several children, that they were all provided for, and she wished to be in the neighbourhood of London, for the purpose of educating the children—I said it was necessary that I should have a reference—she said she could give me twenty references, but she thought she could not do better than to give me a reference to the brother of her late landlord at Bath, under whom she had lived five or six years, who was a builder at Fulham, and she named a man of
the name of Hobbs, who was building some houses at Fulham, and said I should find him at Stamford Brook-lodge—I drove to Fulham, and found some houses being built, and upon inquiry I found the prisoner Hobbs—I told him the object of my visit, and he said he knew Mrs. Norman very well—that she was a widow lady, and had been residing at Bath—that she had had a house of his brother's five or six years, and that she had always paid the rent punctually—I told him my object in asking the reference was, that she was about to take a house of me at 96 l. or 97 l. a year, and I asked him if she was a person in those circumstances that would warrant that proceeding—he said, yes, she was, and that he only knew one thing against her, and that was, that she was rather near in money matters—he either said "near" or "close"—Mrs. Norman called on me the next morning or the morning after, and in consequence of what Hobbs had said, I let her the house.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Are you sure that Hobbs said this? A. Yes, quite sure—I got the key back in about ten days.
JOHN LOWE . I am a grocer and live at Acton—that is about two miles and a half from Mr. Tubbs's cottage, at Harlesdon Green—on the 18th of August last, I saw the prisoner Norman—she told me her name was Norman, and she had just taken Mr. Tubbs's cottage—she came to me for tea and sugar, and grocery—I supplied her with goods that day to the amount of 30 s., and I continued to serve her on the following week—I know the prisoner Foster—he was in the habit of coming to my shop frequently for goods of the same description as Mrs. Norman had—I remember on the Saturday after Mrs. Norman had come to me, Foster came and ordered some goods—he had walked down, and he wished me to give him a ride back again in my cart, which I did—he admired the horse, and said his mistress had got a pair of horses, a very fine strapping pair, sixteen hands high, that they had ordered hay and corn of Mr. Billington, a neighbour of mine, that the coachman and cook were coming on Monday, and I was to be sure to bring my bill in on Monday, and it would be paid on the Tuesday—the same afternoon the prisoner Grafton came for more goods, and it being a wet afternoon, she requested a ride back also, and I took her in the
