cart—I rather grumbled about going up so often with the things, it being a great distance, and wished them to give me the order in the morning and I would execute it—she told me her mistress came from Bath, and the cook was down there then—that she was coming up on Monday, and then she would come with the orders, and would not give me so much trouble-Grafton said she had lived with her mistress six years—when Mrs. Norman came to the shop she wanted every article good, but did not say any thing about price—but Grafton said her mistress was not very particular about price so long as the things were good—the amount of the goods they had of me was 14 l. 6 s. 8 d.—it began on the 18th of August and ended on the 27th—they had of me tea, sugar, butter, soap, blacking, bacon, candles, and other things, and a great many things which I did not sell, I went and got for them.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDEKHAST. Q. Did you not ask for her custom? A. No, sir—she said she was the lady that had taken Mr. Tubbs's cottage, and I said I should be happy to have her custom—she told me she had got Mr. Tubbs's cottage, and I went to Mr. Tubbs's, but it was not from what he said that I trusted her—I had trusted her before I saw him, but not to the whole of this amount—she came to me on a Saturday, and I saw Mr. Tubbs one day in the following week.
COURT. Q. Should you have trusted her if she had not been at that house, and if you had not heard from the servants about the horses and other things? A. No, I should not.
JOSEPH CLARK . I am a journeyman butcher, in the service of Mr. May, near Harelsdon-green. He supplied goods to the two servants, Grafton and Foster—I gave them some meat and some grocery things—I do not know to what amount, nor for how long—I delivered several joints of meat.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. You went to the house? A. We sent down, and took orders.
JAMES POPE . I live at Harlesdon-green, and am a milkman. I know the prisoner Norman—I do not know the other female prisoner's name, but she was the person who was in the house, and I know Foster—they came to live in Mr. Tubbs's house on the 18th of August, I think—I sent to the women in care of the house to say that I should be happy to serve the lady, and then they sent to me, I think, but I was not at home—I supplied them with cream, and butter, and eggs, up to the 27th of that month, to the amount of 1 l. 17 s., odd-Norman left the cottage on Monday night, the 27th—I saw her in the morning of that day, and took my bill—she said I had done quite right to bring my bill, and she would settle with me on the morrow morning—I saw Grafton and Foster there, acting as servants—I asked Foster what his mistress's name was—he told me her name was Norman, that she came from Bath, that she was a widow, that there was a carriage and horses, and daughters, and that he expected them to arrive every day—I went up on the Tuesday morning, and they were all gone—I had taken them a roasting pig on the Sunday morning—I took it up in a dish, and delivered it to Grafton—I asked her for the dish, and she said I might leave it; and one day, when I took some butter, I left a basket there, which is a usual thing-people sometimes say, "You may leave it," and we do.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDSRGAST. Q. You do not blame them for giving you their custom when you call for it? A. No—I did not go to them for custom myself, but my mother did—I was not at home when the lady arrived, but my mother was let know that the lady had come, and then she went-Foster did not tell me that the horses were sixteen hands high—he did not say he had seen them—he did not say any thing to show that he had not just been engaged.
WILLIAM BILLINGDON . I am a corn-dealer. My account of goods supplied to Mrs. Norman, at Mr. Tubbs's cottage, between the 21st and 25th of August last, was 10 l. 0 s. 4 d.—I never saw Mrs. Norman myself—after they left the cottage I fetched the sacks away—they were corn sacks, marked with my own name—I found them after they left in the kitchen—there did not appear to have been any horse there at all—there was a small quantity of straw in the coach-house place, or stable, which had been packed up with other goods.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. You sent somebody to ask for their custom directly, did you not? A. Yes.
HENRY HOLFORD . I am a boot and shoemaker, and live at Acton. In consequence of a communication made to me by Mr. Billingdon's man, I went to the cottage at Harlesdon-green. I saw there the prisoner Grafton, who was acting in the capacity of a servant—she wanted me to measure her for a pair of shoes if I could get them done by Saturday—it was on
Wednesday or Thursday, the 22nd or 23rd of August, that I went—I promised to get them done, and measured her—she asked me if I had a card—she said the family were coming on Friday, and most likely she could get me their custom, and they would be good customers, as they were a large family—on the Saturday morning I sent the shoes—they were a pair of Spanish slippers-in the afternoon Grafton came to my shop and wanted me to go over there in the evening to measure their young ladies for some shoes—she said the family was come—she looked in my window and said if I had a pair or two made I might take them with me, and I was to be over there by seven o'clock—I went over, and took with me seven or eight pairs of shoes and some boots and clogs—I saw Grafton—she took the shoes and boots and clogs into another room, Mrs. Norman came out of the room and said I was behind my time, and one of the family was gone to bed, but if I would leave the goods, if either pair fitted she would return the others by Mr. Billingdon's cart on the Monday morning—she said there was none small enough for one little girl, she would get me to measure her for a pair and bring them the beginning of the week—I left my goods and never got any of them back.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. How old was the little girl? A. I do not know, perhaps about eight years old—I did not have the little girl taken up—I offered to go after my goods, but Mrs. Norman promised to send them back on the Monday—I left three pain—I should have been glad to have sold as many as I could if I had got the money for them—I did not want to trust them—I did not expect that all the three pairs were for one person—I saw two small children.
JAMES WARD . I am journeyman to Mr. Holford. I remember receiving a note from Foster while they were living at the cottage—he brought a note, as he said, from Mrs. Norman, for a pair of shoes—I opened the note and let him have a pair—this is the note—( read)—"Mrs. Norman requests Mr. Holford to let the bearer, her servant, have a pair of boots or shoes, which he has that will fit, and a pair of walking boots or shoes, the size sent—the young man will explain."
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. If the size had suited he might have had more? A. Yes.
JOHN PONTIFEX . I am a copper-smith, and live in Shoe-lane, In September last, I had a house and fixtures at Winchmore-hill—I advertised it in September—the prisoner Norman called in the name of Mary Ann Norman, on the 22nd of September, and said she had seen my house which was to let at Winchmore-hill, and thought it would suit her, if we could agree for terms—I said my terms were 40 l. a-year, but I expected the fixtures to be paid for—she said, if the fixtures went in with the rent, she should have no objection to take it—I told her I should have no objection to that, if I had a good reference—she said she should take it for three years, and she would give me a good reference—she paused a little, as though considering who she should name, and she said, "Mr. Pontifex, I think I cannot name better than my old landlord, that I have rented under many years, which is Mr. Hobbs, a builder, in Stamford Villa, Fulham-road"—she begged I would send as soon as possible, as she had another house in view—I sent that day-in consequence of what my young man told me, I agreed with her on the 24th for three years, at 40 l. a-year—she occupied my cottage from eight to nine days—on the 8th of October, I received a letter with the key-before taking the house, she asked me if
there were good tradesmen about Winchmore-hill—I said yes—she asked if there were good coal-merchants—I told her there were persons that could serve her with coals—she asked who was my coal-merchant—I said Dyer—she said, would I be so kind as to order some for her, and I ordered a ton, which were sent in the next day—she asked who was my wine-merchant, and I told her what little I had, I had of a person at Enfield—she asked me his name, and I said Smith—she asked if there were any good grocers—I said yes, one very good shop—she asked where I dealt—I told her at Mr. Barrough's, at Hatton-garden—after that, Mr. Barrough called on me—I went on the 9th of October, to see the house at Winchmore-hill—the fixtures were left, but no furniture.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. You did not want furniture? A. No, I was glad to get my house and fixtures, but I should rather have had a good tenant.
JOHN SEARLES LADE . I am clerk to Mr. Pontifex. In consequence of directions from him, I went to the prisoner Hobbs, at Stamford Villa, in the Fulham-road—I saw him, and asked him if he knew a lady by the name of Norman—he said he did—I asked him if she was likely to pay the rent of a house of 40 l. or 50 l. a-year—he said yes, she was a very respectable' person—that he had a very good reference from his brother, who lived at Southampton—he said that I had no cause to be afraid of the rent, as that would be always certain, and I had no cause to go twice for it, that she kept a phaeton and pair—he said she was very close in her dealing—I communicated to Mr. Pontifex what he told me.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Where was it you saw Hobbs?— A. At Stamford Villa, Fulham-road—it was an unfinished house—I did not count how many unfinished houses there were—I should think they would let for about 40 l. a-year—there were workmen engaged upon them—the conversation lasted about ten minutes perhaps—he was not doing any thing—I met him at the door.
THOMAS OSLIFFE . I am in partnership with John Udall of Winchmore-hill. We are grocers and general-dealers—On the 29th of September, the prisoner Norman came to our shop—she gave the name of Norman, and ordered several articles, which were sent down—here is our bill, amounting to 5 l. 10 s. 1 d.—she said she had taken a house of Mr. Pontifex, and she would pay for the articles as we sent them down if we chose, but that she generally paid weekly—she had a book, and we entered several things—the prisoner Foster fetched several articles—the book was cast up on Saturday night, and they were to be paid for on the Monday—Foster came, I believe, every day—I made up the book by the direction of Foster—he said he only knew the family since they came there—I was to go down on the Monday with the bill, but Mr. Pontifex's servant came to tell us they were gone—there was 1lb. weight of tea, 3lbs. weight of sugar, blacking, brooms, &c., and such like things.
JOHN SELL . I live at Winchmore-hill, and am a carpenter and iron-monger. I know the two female prisoners, and Foster—on Monday the 1st of October, I saw Foster sweeping the gravel walk of the house at Winchmore-hill, where Norman lived—I asked him if the lady was within, and if I could see her—he said, "Go to the door if you please, and you will see the servant"—I went and saw Grafton—I asked if I could see the lady—she said, "You cannot see her for an hour, if you come again you can see her"—I went, and Mrs. Norman made her appearance—I asked her if there
was anything wanted in the carpentering line—she said she had received a card from Mr. Pontifex, and that I was the person she should employ, and she ordered two deal boxes to be made—she showed me a box standing on a chair—they were to be made similar to that, only larger, deeper, and a drawer at the bottom of each—she said she should wish to have them as early as I could possibly get them done—she said she wanted an ironing-board made and fixed in the scullery, and a clothes-horse and window-blind—she said there would be a corn-bin wanting, but she must have the other orders executed first—she told Foster to show me some jobs that were wanted to be done in the stable, Foster went with me to the stable, and gave me orders to make a couple of shelves ten feet long, to go behind the door—he wanted them in the course of the week—I asked him if they wanted any thing in the ironmongery way, and Grafton told me there were many things that were wanted in that line, and the would come up to the shop, and give me the order—in about a quarter of an hour after I returned home, Grafton came to the shop, and brought a list of the articles that she wanted—there were pails for the stable and house, dripping-pans, safes, and other things, amounting to 2 l. 17 s. 9 d.—I delivered them the same evening—she gave me the measure by a piece of string for a brass fender, and fire irons, and a tea-kettle were ordered—I saw the dripping-pan afterwards in the cart, going away—I saw the house several times after they had left—there were none of these things left in the house or stable.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Who first applied to you? A. I applied myself.
WILLIAM STILLING . I live at No. 11, New Inn-yard, Shoreditch. I know Norman and Guy—Norman came to me as his wife—they went by the name of Mr. and Mrs. Thompson—I had a house, No. 15, Bridge water-square, that was to let—Thompson and the woman came about a fortnight before last Michaelmas—Thompson said he thought the house would suit him—he asked what rent it was—I said, "58 l. a year"—I wanted 10 l. for fixtures—he said he would not pay for any fixtures, he would give me the rent—he took it for three years—he said his goods were coming from Manchester, and he was in the Manchester trade—upon this arrangement I asked for a reference, and Thompson wrote down this reference for me—this is what he wrote in my presence and hers—( read)—"Mr. Hobbs, builder, Eagle New Tap, Manor-street, Chelsea"—I went to Mr. Hobbs, and saw him at the Eagle Tap, in New Manor-street—I asked him if he knew Mr. Thompson—he said yes, very well, he was a very respectable man, he had a good deal of property, and lived in a house of his brother's at Manchester for ten years, and always paid his rent very well—I asked him if he would be afraid to let him a house of 50 l. or 60 l. a-year—he said not for 100 l. a year, he should be sure of the rent—he said I need not be afraid of letting him the house, I should be sure of the rent—in consequence of that I gave him possession of the house with Norman, who had passed as his wife—I have not got possession of the house now—there are some of the children in it now.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDEROAST. Q. Your rent is not due? A. No, I have not demanded any rent—some person came and took away a great quantity of goods from the house—there are five young children there—the eldest says she is seventeen years old—the others are much younger—I am sure Norman was there when he said he was her husband—I will
undertake to say that—I saw her with him two or three times, and when they came and took possession.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. When was it you went to Hobbs? A. About ten days before Michaelmas—I saw him at the bar of the Eagle tap—it is in a street going out of the King's-road.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Had they possession of your house from just before Michaelmas up to the present time? A. Yes—there was no furniture in the house—there are two or three old chairs and a table, and something of that kind.
WILLIAM STEVENS . I am a slater, and live at No. 16, Charles-street, Middlesex Hospital. I have known Grafton and Foster about four years—they have always gone with me by the name of Thompson.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Do you keep a house? A. I have got a house—I do not keep the house there.
JOSEPH HIGGS re-examined. I took Norman into custody on the 5th of November—Hobbs was taken on the 19th—there had been an examination of Norman before Hobbs was taken.
(George Smallwood, a builder, at Walham Green; William Shepherd, of Lisson-street, Lisson Grove; George Upward, Kent-road, builder, and—Rule, of the Lion brewery, Coppice-row, gave the prisoner Hobbs a good character.)
NORMAN— GUILTY . Aged 49.
GUY— GUILTY . Aged 48.
Confined Two Years.
HOBBS— GUILTY . Aged 58.
GRAFTON— GUILTY . Aged 21.
FOSTER— GUILTY . Aged 18.
Confined Six Months.
Fourth Jury, before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
[trialAccount t18381126-218]
218. JOHN GREENO was indicted for a misdemeanor.
MR. HORRY conducted the Prosecution.
SARAH WARE . I live in Gravel-lane, and am the wife of James Ware, a seafaring man—he has been absent a considerable time. I have known the prisoner a good while by coming down to the King William—he represented that he knew the law—I had a young man named Moore in trouble, and the prisoner said he would do all he could for me—I was at Lambeth-street police-office, when Moore was examined—the prisoner came up to a young woman who was with me just outside Lambeth-street office—Moore was at that time in custody—the prisoner spoke to her, and asked me what I was crying about—I said, "About a young man that was in prison"—he asked me what about—I said, "About a watch"—I saw him again after that—we went to a public-house and had some porter—I saw him again on the 8th of October, which was a week afterwards—he asked me to go with him to get the depositions—I paid him on that occasion three shillings—he told me to come up to the Old Bailey with him, as he was going to get two subpoenas—I went home to get some money, and then returned down here—I got some money—he asked me for 5 s. to get two subpoenas—I gave it him—he went away for seven or eight minutes—he then returned and said he could not get it without I gave him another shilling—I gave him the shilling to finish paying for the two subpoenas—he said it was for that purpose—he returned with two long pieces of paper and a piece of parchment—the papers appeared to be new ones—they were quite
clean—the parchment appeared to be quite clean—it was straight, not folded at all—he gave me the parchment, and put the two pieces of paper in his coat pocket—I still stopped at the George public-house, Old Bailey—I folded the parchment up at the George, and put it in my bosom—after that I went to the King William, at Shad well—the prisoner asked me to let him look at the parchment to show to a person—I gave it him—I saw him again the next morning—I came up again to the George—before we went into the George, I said, "Where is that piece of paper I gave to you last night?"—he said he had given it to me—I told him he had not—when we got to the George, he asked me for 4 s. 6 d. to get another original, as the other one was lost—he said he must get a new one—I gave him two half-crowns in order to get a new one—I believed what he said, and that was the reason why I parted with my money—he went away—he told me I could go with him if I liked—I came into the Court, and he left me at the bottom of some stone stairs—I saw him again in about two minutes—he said, "It is all right, I have got one"—I went back to the George—after that he showed me a piece of parchment, and said he had got it—that piece was folded the same way, as far as I can judge, as I had folded the one the night before—it seemed to me to be the same, because it was rather dirty—the piece of parchment that I returned him the night before was dirty, by being folded and putting it down my bosom—I gave the money to the prisoner for the purpose of getting another, and no other purpose whatever.
JOHN JONAS . I am a clerk in the office of Mr. Clark. It is my duty to deliver subpoenas to persons applying for them—I have referred to the list of subpoenas issued last Sessions—I find one subpoena and two copies issued in the name of Moore, and that is the only one issued in that Sessions in that name—the charge for a subpoena is 2 s. 6 d., and the two copies 1 s., which would be 3 s. 6 d.—I should think these two copies were issued from our office—I filled up the subpoena—I did not issue more than one.
Cross-examined by MR. PATNE. Q. You delivered one original subpoena and two copies? A. Yes—I keep an account of all the subpoenas I issue.
MR. PAYNE to SARAH WARE. Q. Who was the subpoena given to? A. I did not see him give it to any body—he did not give it to Mr. Wiggins—Mr. Wiggins looked at it—when he came down the stone stairs he said he had got a new one, and when he got to the George, he laid the parchment on the table, and Mr. Wiggins looked over it, but did not take it in his hand—it was folded in the same way as the first—I had kept it about two or three hours the night before—I said it was dirty when he showed it to me afterwards—I was sitting on one side of the table in the tap-room, and he on the other—he did not pull out a piece of parchment and give it to Mr. Wiggins—I have never said so—I am twenty-two years old—I was married twelve months ago, at Stepney-church—my husband staid about two months with me—I have never seen him since—he is on board the Donegal—he does not send me any of his pay—Moore was a friend of mine—I have never had any quarrel with the prisoner—I was not angry with him on account of Moore being convicted—he never laid out any money for me to pay for any cab —when he asked for the 4 s. 6 d. he said he was going to get a new original—the waiter and I, and another young woman, and Mr. Wiggins's brother, were in the public-house with the prisoner—I was at four or five different public-houses with the prisoner—he went into the prison to Moore, at my desire, to take him a clean shirt—Mr. Wiggins, the
brother of the other Mr. Wiggins, Was the person I employed in the last case.
MR. HORRY. Q. When did you first make an agreement with Wiggins about getting counsel? A. After the prisoner said he had lost the original—I was present when Moore was tried—the witnesses did not appear for him.
JURY. Q. When were you first acquainted with the prisoner? A. Down at Lambeth-street—but I had seen him at the King William before—I have always treated him at the public-houses I have been at with him.
SAMUEL WIGGINS . I am a tailor, and live at No. 6, Sidney-place, Sidney-street, City-road—I was in the George on Thursday, the 18th of October, and saw the prosecutrix, the prisoner, and another young woman—I saw the prisoner come in, stand at the comer of the box, and ask for 4 s. 6 d., to get a subpoena, in the room of the one he had lost the day before—she gave him two half-crowns—he said, "You may as well give me a crown," as she had only got the two half-crowns—they both went out—he came back in ten minutes or a quarter of an hour—the friend who was with the prosecutrix asked him if he had got it—he said yes, it was all right—he threw it on the table, and the prosecutrix said, "In my opinion this is the one that I folded up last night"—she said she thought it was the same she had given him the night before—I said, "What writing is on the back of this?"—he said, "You b——y fool, cannot you read?"—I said, "No, nor any body else."
Cross-examined. Q. How came you to be there? I had made a pair of trowsers for a gentleman—I do not interfere in the defence of prisoners, my brother does, and when he is not here, I sometimes give answers and sometimes go down to the George—the prosecutrix did not give the subpoena to me—I have not had a quarrel with the prisoner—I never saw him before then—I did not tell the Magistrate this—I was not asked—I looked at the back of the paper—I have worked for Mr. Berdoe, of Goswell-road since last April—I made part of a great coat with another man—we work at home, because he has not convenience on his premises.
PHOEBE FORDHAM . I am bar-maid at the Mahogany Bar public-house, in Wellclose-square—before the last Old Bailey Sessions the prisoner called and asked if I remembered seeing two men, and serving a glass of rum-and-water the week before, and whether the old man was drunk—I said "Yes, he was drunk"—he asked if I would attend at the Sessions—I said no, I could not—he said, if I would not attend by fair means he would make me, and in the evening he brought down a subpoena, and left it—this is the subpoena—I then said I could not attend—he said if he could do without me he would, but he would let me know—this subpoena is the same with respect to the writing as when I received it—it has got dirty since.
MARY PYKE . I am a widow, and live at No. 17, Gray's-alley, Wellclose-square, and am a haberdasher. One day, before the last Old Bailey Sessions, the prisoner came and asked me if two men were in my shop on Saturday night—I said, "What did they want?"—he said, it was not Saturday last, it was Saturday week—I said, "What did they want to buy?"—he said, "Nothing"—he said it was a coloured man and a sailor—he asked me to go to the Old Bailey, on behalf of Thomas Moore—I said I was a widow with four children, it was inconvenient for me to attend—he said if I would not attend he knew what to do—he brought me a paper previous
to that, and wanted me to sign it, which I refused to do—he afterwards gave me this subpoena—I did not attend.
JAMES BAKER . I am potman at the George, Old Bailey. I remember the prosecutrix and the prisoner being at our house before the last Sessions—I saw the prisoner receive 6 s., but I heard no observation.
ROBERT DAVIS . I am an officer of Lambeth-street Police-office. I apprehended the prisoner on Friday, the 9th of November—I asked him if he knew a man of the name of Moore, who was committed from our office last Sessions for stealing a watch—he said, "Yes"—I told him he must consider himself in custody for having obtained some money from a woman, a friend of Moore, to obtain subpoenas, and draw out a brief—he said, "Is that all? I got the subpoenas at Mr. Clark's office, and can prove it"—I then told him he was charged with having obtained money for a second subpoena, having stated he had lost the first—he said, "That is not true, but I suppose it was some money that she lent me at the Jolly Sailor."
SARAH WARE re-examined. I never lent the prisoner any money at the Jolly Sailor—I lent him half-a-crown and one shilling at the King William, on the 17th of October, the day he got the subpoen A.
JURY. Q. Did he state for what purpose he borrowed that money? A. No; he said he would give it me before he went away in the evening.
GUILTY . Aged 28.— Confined One Year.
[trialAccount t18381126-219]
219. EDWARD WICKHAM was indicted for a misdemeanor.
MR. BALLANTINE conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM WALKER . I live in Upper Smith-street, Northampton-square. I have known the prisoner nine or ten months—he was introduced as Captain Frew—he stated he was in the 35th Madras Infantry—some time ago he asked me to lend him 8 l. on an £8 bill, for ten days—I consented to do so—he promised to pay it himself, if I would hold the bill till that time—I was to give Thomas Transfield, on account of that bill, 7 l. 5 s., and give the captain the other 15 s.—Transfield introduced him to me as Captain Frew, and the captain promised to make me a present when the bill was paid—that bill was never paid—on the 6th of November he drew another bill—I produce that—it was drawn by the prisoner for 21 l., and the former bill was cancelled—I advanced him 8 l. 15 s. more, besides the money for the first bill—this is for 21 l., dated November 6th, signed "E. W. Frew"—he drew this bill in my presence—before it became due he brought this cheque—he then wished me to take my bill out, and give him the remainder—this is a cheque for 52 l. 8 s. 6 d., signed by George Duncan, to Messrs. Coborn and Co., No. 4, Whitehall, dated from Liverpool, October 29th—I said I had not money enough in the house, and I would not do it till I had been to the bank—I went myself to the banker's with it, in the state in which it was—when I advanced the prisoner the 8 l., I did it under the impression that he was a Captain Frew, of the East India Company's service—I always thought he was nothing else—I advanced the money to him, believing the promissory note was good and genuine—I have never received a farthing of the money I lent him, neither the first nor second.
Cross-examined by MR. PRICE. Q. How long have you known him? A. Nine or ten months—I first became acquainted with him by going to the Eagle tavern, City-road, to have a glass of gin and water, or a pint of beer—Transfield
introduced him to me there as Captain Frew—I never heard any other appellation at the tavern—I know that the captain lived at Windsor-terrace, City-road, where the bill was made payable—I do not know whose house he lived at—I never inquired—I knew where he lived—all these representations occurred at the Eagle-tavern—I advanced my money considerably on the personal appearance and respectability of Captain Frew—I cannot tell the day that he first asked me for the 8 l.—it was about two months ago, or perhaps not so much—it was about three weeks before the 6th of November I advanced 7 l. 5 s. to Transfield, and 15 s. to him, by his order—I never had a written order to pay that 7 l. 5 s.—I had nothing but the bill—I did not tell Transfield that 15 s. were deducted for the interest for the eight days—I told him I detained 15 s. back for the captain—I did not keep 15 s. back for interest—I paid 15 s. to the captain the day after I paid the 7 l. 5 s. to Transfield, at three o'clock—I do not keep any books, nor does my wife—my wife receives the rents I live on, if I am not in the way—I have four houses, two at Camberwell, and two at Clerkenwell—I was not to receive any weekly payment for this money—the £8 bill was at ten days' date—he burnt it himself in my presence, at the time he gave me the £21 bill—it had become due about a day or two before—he did not draw it at the time I advanced Transfield the money—I paid him the next 8 l. on the 6th of November, about ten days after I paid him the 15 s.—only my wife and myself were present—I paid him the 8 l. in the same place, and by myself—when he drew this bill he said, "I shall make you a handsome present"—he gave me the £21 bill—I do not know whether he meant to give me the 5 l. or not—if he had paid the 2 l. I should have returned him the change, but I should have taken the legal interest, and not more—that is not my usual way of doing business—I may charge more, but I considered the captain a safe man—I have never given any other money to persons in names that I knew were not theirs—I have never lent a gentleman money, and taken his account in a name that I knew was not his—I might not find it out.
COURT. Q. Do you mean to say that you have not done so? A. I have not—I have never lent money for the sake of usury.
Cross-examined by MR. PRICE. Q. You mean to swear now, on your oath that you never have lent any gentleman frequenting that tavern money in a name which you knew not to be his proper name? A. No, not that I know of—I do not know a gentleman named Reed, unless you mean Mr. Reed Baldwin—he is a singer—I do not know that Baldwin is a singing name—I do not know that his name is Reed, and not Baldwin—he has never told me that his real name was Reed, and not Baldwin—it is not correct if Mr. Baldwin should state that when he borrowed my money he told me his name was Reed, and I preferred to have it in the name of Baldwin—Baldwin introduced me to the prisoner as Captain Frew—he told me in the coffee-house that he was Captain Frew—Transfield also introduced him to me—they both did—he brought Captain Frew, and said, "This is Captain Frew, who wants to borrow some money of you"—that was some months after I knew Captain Frew—I cannot tell how long—he came himself, and asked me to lend him some money—I believe he came to me to borrow money for Tom Transfield, and not for himself, because Transfield had it from me—I believe he did not borrow the 8 l. for himself, but he chose to give me his security—I heard that the money was wanted to pay Transfield's rent—I do not know why that rent was not paid—I understood the captain had it
back again from Transfield's father—I do not know how much—I believe it was 6 l. 19 s. 6 d.—I do not think that it was in consequence of the captain getting so large a portion of that back, that he wanted to borrow the second 8 l. to pay him again—I do not know that the second 8 l. was borrowed to repay the first—I believe the second 8 l. was borrowed to repay Transfield—I suppose so—he did not tell me so—none of them told me—I believed it from report—from the captain himself—I have never heard from Transfield for what purpose it was borrowed—I do not know whose writing this letter is—( looking at one)—the cheque was tendered to me by the captain before the bill became due, and that I refused to give cash for—I called at Windsor-terrace once, or not more than twice—I have never seen him or his wife go in there—I knew where he lodged, but he always represented himself as a single man—I know nothing of him, except this casual acquaintance in the coffee-room.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Whether this money was lent for Transfield or for the prisoner's own purposes, have you received any of it since? A. None at all—I should not have lent the money without the bill given me by the prisoner—I believed the description of the prisoner upon the face of the note was a true description—I should not have lent a shilling of either of these sums if I had not believed that was correct.
WILLIAM ARCHER SHEE . I reside in Cavendish-square, and am in the Military Secretary's office in the East India House. No one of the prisoner's name appears to be in their service, supposing the lists to be correct, which we have every reason to imagine they are—I have made every possible inquiry on this subject—there is a Captain Robert Frew on the retired list of the Madras army—there is no one with the initials "E. W. Frew."
Cross-examined. Q. You do not undertake to say there is no such person in the service? A. I can only give my testimony from the information I possess—when a gentleman gets leave to come home if he stops beyond five years, he is out of the service by Act of Parliament, and he is not on the retired list—I have not inquired respecting the naval list—I have not searched the pension list—I have searched the retired list, which is the same as far as the names go.
RICHARD HAWKES ( police-constable N 40.) On the 19th of November I went to No. 8, Windsor-terrace, City-road, to look for the person who calls himself Captain Frew—I saw a female at the door—I went up stairs in search of him, and on the third floor I found the prisoner—I told him he was wanted on a charge of obtaining money under false pretences—he asked to go to his apartments to change his dress, and he did so—as I was going up stairs I heard a noise in the third floor—I went up, and the prisoner had fallen down from a chair, in attempting to make his escape through a trap-door—he told me if he could have made his escape he would.
( Bill read)—"21 l. 0 s. 0 d. Nov. 6, 1838. Ten days after date, I promise to pay Mr. William Walker, or order, the sum of 21 l. for value received. E. W. FREW, Capt. E. I. C. S. M. N. L"
MR. PRICE called
THOMAS TRANSFIELD . I am a waiter at the Eagle tavern. I have known the prisoner ten or twelve months—he has frequented the house—on a late occasion I asked him to assist me in obtaining a loan, to oblige a part of my family, to save their goods from being seized—at that time Mr. Frew bad the note—I said, "I dare say Mr. Walker will advance the money for
him"—he said, "Very well"—I said that from knowing that Mr. Walker was in the habit of doing these things—I cannot read—Walker paid the money to me on the morning that he got the note from Mr. Frew—he paid me 7 l. 5 s.—he kept 15 s. back for the interest—I think he said he borrowed it for fourteen days—I understood he would keep the bill himself—I took that money to my father—he did not pay the rent with it, on account of the landlord being out of town—I was given to understand that my father lent the money to the captain again—in a few days the captain applied to Mr. Walker for more—my father was pressing for the money again.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. What is the name of that person who has just left the court? A. He goes by the name of Baldwin—that is only his professional name—I was intimate with the prisoner no further than his using the coffee-room as a customer—I have been servant there about two years and three months—I asked him for the money as he was a customer—I could make more free with this gentleman than any other person—I cannot tell my reason—I was not on intimate terms with him—I was in the habit of seeing him out of doors from the bar to the saloon—I used not to walk about with him—I have met him when I have been going for pump water—I have seen him at his own house—I went with a letter once from Mrs. Rouse, my mistress—he was not intimate with my master and mistress, that I know of—I went to his house again, to take him a brace of birds that I bought for him, about six months ago—I did not make him a present of them—I did not think well of Mr. Walker, not so well as of the captain—I had no reason to think ill of him then—I believed him to be an honest man at the time I introduced my friend the captain to him—I know nothing further of him than borrowing money of him—Mr. Walker and I had had transactions together, two for myself and one for another person—I did introduce more than one to him—he behaved so well as to lend me money when I wanted it—I introduced another person to him to borrow money—as far as I know his conduct was all correct—I had no other reason than to believe him to be an honest, respectable man—7 l. 5 s. was paid by him to me at the Bird-cage, at the corner of Silver-street, Wood-street—I went to the landlord to pay the rent with it—my wife's father was with me—my landlord was not at home—he had been pressing for rent about a week or a fortnight before—the last time he applied was a week ago—I did not see him the day I had the money from Mr. Walker, but his clerk would not take it without I paid some taxes, which I would net—my wife's father went with me—the money went from me to my father-in-law's hands some three or four days after he told me he had given it back to Captain Frew—my father-in-law was present when I received the 7 l. 5 s.—I do not know that he had the 15 s. for interest.
JURY. Q. Has that rent never been paid? A. It has, within the last week—my landlord was very pressing.
WILLIAM REED BALDWIN . My real name is William John Reed—it is a very general thing among theatricals to assume a name. I have recently had money transactions with Mr. Walker, who frequents the Eagle tavern—he was introduced to me as a person who lends money, by a person on the establishment—the original sum lent was 10 l.—I was to repay by instalments of 7 s. a week, to give a promissory note for 15 l. 10 s.—I told him when I went to sign the note my name was not Baldwin—it was only my
theatrical name—he said, "I know, but I know you by that name, and I shall take it."
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. What are you? A. Chorus-master at the Eagle—I have known the prisoner some time—I never heard that he was apprehended on a charge from Mr. Tennant—I heard it was Mr. Walker's charge—I have known the prisoner two years—I knew where he lived—I did not visit him—I never knew him in any regiment—I have heard say that he was in the East India Company's service, but whether he told me so I cannot tell.
MR. PRICE. The prisoner was always accosted and addressed by the name of Captain Frew? A. Yes, always.
LUKE WILLIAM WYMARK . I am a bookseller. I have known the prisoner three years—he bore a most unexceptionable character.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Did he lodge at your house? A. No, he resided at my house, and paid me 3 s. a month in advance at first, but afterwards it was in arrears—he never went by the title of Captain Frew, but by the name of Wickham—he never represented that he was a captain in the East India Company's service—I took him to be a military man.
— KNOX. I live at No. 8, Windsor-terrace. The prisoner lodged with me for thirteen or fourteen months, till he was taken—he had a good character, and behaved like a gentleman.
MR. BALLANTINS. Q. Is Captain Frew married? A. I do not know any such man as Captain Frew—I never saw him to my knowledge.
JAMES WALMSLEY . I live in Tysoe-street, Wilmington-square. The prisoner lived with me for about a year and a half—he had a character of the strictest integrity.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. When did he leave you? A. About two years ago—he went by the name of Wickham.
GUILTY . Aged.— Confined Twelve Months.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
ESSEX CASE.
[trialAccount t18381126-220]
220. THOMAS INGLETON was indicted for stealing, on the 29th of October, 1 coat, value 3 l., the goods of George Read.
GEORGE READ . I am ostler to Mr. Bottom, of Windmill-street, Westminster. On the 29th of October I was returning to town with a horse, and stopped at the Red Lion tap at Ilford—I gave my horse to the landlady's son to hold—I had a coat attached to the saddle—I came out in a quarter of an hour, and it was gone—I have not seen it since, but I saw the prisoner with the horse coming up to the door—he had taken the horse from the boy that I gave it to—he gave the horse to the boy, and the boy ran into the house, and said my coat was gone—I am sure the prisoner is the man.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Were you sober? A. Yes—I had three cups of tea there—this was a chesnut-horse, rather high-couraged—he had thrown me off in going down to Chelmsford—the prisoner came into the tap-room, and sat down, and was taken into custody there—I asked him why he took the liberty of taking my horse from the door, and
told him my coat was gone—he asked if I had any mark on it—I said "Yes"—he said, "You have lost the coat, mark and all," and I had him taken—the little boy had got the horse outside while we were talking.
THOMAS HALES . I held the horse. I saw the coat on it—the prisoner came out and asked me to let him have a ride—I told him he had better not, but he rode it five or six minutes—I ran by the side, and picked up a stick, and gave it to him—when I saw he was returning, I came back, and he said, "There is the horse," but the coat was gone then.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you see how far he rode? A. About half a quarter of a mile—I did not go and look for the coat on the road—I am sure I had seen a coat there, it was tied with two pieces of rope—the horse was outside the door two or three minutes, without any one with it.
DANIEL KINGSNORTH . On the 29th of October, Read sent for me to take the prisoner—there was the rope, and the loop in front of the saddle had been cut—I took the prisoner—he swore he would not go—he was with several navigators in the house—I had a desperate struggle to get him out—I was thrown down, and was obliged to call other people.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you say one word before the Magistrate about his swearing be would not go, and about the navigators? A. Yes—I told the Magistrates all the particulars—I believe they took them down—he laid the coat was safe when be brought the horse back.
THOMAS HALES re-examined. The horse had not been at the door alone before I held it—the prisoner came up, and rode away—the coat was safe then—I followed him, and came back two or three minutes before him.
MR. PAYNE. Q. Are you quite sure that you missed the coat at the time he first arrived, or afterwards? A. I missed—it. when he came up—he called me to come out—he came to the bar—he had left the house about a minute then.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Recorder.
KENT CASES.
[trialAccount t18381126-221]
221. DAVID SMALL was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of November, 3 half-crowns, 1 shilling, and 1 fourpenny-piece, the monies of Joseph Stokes.
JOSEPH STOKES . I live in Woolwich, and am in the Royal Artillery. On Saturday, the 1st of November, I placed my jacket on the prisoner's bed, which joins mine, while I made my own bed—it was about a quarter before eight o'clock in the evening—I left my jacket there, when I got into bed—I had in it three half-crowns, one shilling, and one fourpenny-piece, in a wash-leather purse—I missed it the following morning, about half-past six o'clock—twelve or fourteen persons slept in the same room—they would have an opportunity of seeing where I placed my jacket—I asked Mills to look for my jacket, after I got to bed, and then Small flung it on my bed, off his own bed—Mills doubled it up, and put it on the top of the saddle-bags—I have never seen the purse since—it could not have escaped from the jacket when it was thrown—it was thrown but a little way, and if it had
I should have seen it in the morning—it was about five minutes past eight o'clock at night when Small threw it to me—I did not miss the money till half-past six o'clock in the morning—my jacket was on the saddle-bag, at the head of my own bed—there was nothing to prevent any one else getting at it in the night—I was a little the worse for liquor the overnight, but was capable of doing my duty—I did not mention to the prisoner my suspicions till the next morning, after the sergeant-major had told him of it—his showing some money to the men in the room, the night he took it, gave us suspicion.
DAVID MILLS . I am a gunner in the Royal Artillery. On the 1st of November I left the stables about eight o'clock in the evening, and went to bed—I saw Small sitting on the outside of his bed, not in bed—he had his jacket off as he came from the stable—the prosecutor was sitting on his bed—he got up, and came towards the fire, with his jacket in his hand, then he went back to his bed, with the jacket, and laid it somewhere—I do not know where—my bed was next to Small's bed, and Stokes called me to get his jacket for him—I went to the fire to look for it, and could not find it—I was coming back to tell him, and I saw the prisoner, with the jacket in his hand, and his right-hand was in the pocket—I saw the buttons on the sleeve of the jacket—I was coming to the prosecutor, and Small called out, "Here is your jacket, Stokes," he then threw it on his bed and folded it up—I saw Small stoop and take up his own jacket, and put his hand to the pocket of his own jacket, and I heard something jingle as it was falling in, which I thought was money—I got up about a quarter past six o'clock in the morning.
Prisoner. Q. Where were you? A. I was coming to tell Stokes I did not see his jacket by the fire—I saw you sitting on the bed, and the jacket was on your knee at the time—I think there were two candles burning on the table.
TIMOTHY POOLE . I am a driver in the Royal Horse Artillery. I went to these quarters about eight o'clock—I saw the prisoner there—I saw him take 1 s. 6 d. in silver, and 7 1/2 d. in copper out of his own jacket-pocket, before he pulled it off—he put it into his pocket again, and then he west to his bed—in about twenty minutes after he called me to his bed-side, and showed me three half-crowns, one shilling, one fourpenny-piece, and some halfpence, and said it was all his own—there were two beds between his and mine—Stokes's bed was not on the same side—I did not see any purse.
GEORGE PARRY . I am a gunner in the Royal Artillery. I went into the room where the prisoner and the prosecutor slept about ten minutes past eight o'clock—the prisoner had three half-crowns, and 1 s. 3 d.—he said, "Parry, I will give you this money to take care of till the morning, as I don't know who sleeps beside me," and he gave it me—I have got that money.
Prisoner. I gave him my own money—I enlisted for a recruit, and I got the money of the quarter-master—that they all knew. Witness. He had been about a month in the regiment—he would receive ten shillings, and he had paid the quarter-master half-a-crown.
Prisoner. I got this money, and came home after I had done drill—I laid my jacket on the bed—it was thrown off, and the money fell out—I picked it up, and put it into my own pocket—it was my own money. Witness. I did not know him till he enlisted, which is about a month ago—the
prisoner's jacket was not searched—the following morning he was taken off at the guard-room—I was not the worse for liquor.
TIMOTHY POOLE re-examined. I saw him with a fourpenny-piece—I was not the worse for liquor.
Prisoner. Mills did not say any thing that night, hut the next day he said he saw my hand in the pocket.
DAVID MILLS re-examined. I thought I could put more dependence in my comrade than to think he would do that—I did not think he Was taking my money—I heard the money ring, but I did not like to interfere with the man—I did not suppose that he had taken any money till I heard it ring—I did not know whether Stokes had money.
JURY. Q. Bid you see a purse? A. No—the prisoner was not charged with this till a quarter past seven o'clock in the morning, after he had been to the stable.
Prisoner. I did not carry any jacket with me to the stable—I could not have had it in my pocket. Witness. You would have pockets in your trowsers too.
GUILTY . Aged 18.— Judgment Respited.
[trialAccount t18381126-222]
222. ROBERT AMBROSE was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of November, 6lbs. weight of chain, value 1 l., the goods of Charles Yewan, his master.
CHARLES YEWAN . I am a stone-mason, and live at Parson's-hill, Woolwich. I lost a sling-chain, which was safe on my premises on the 8th of November—the prisoner was in my employ as a labourer—I did not miss it till I received information, and found it at the constable's house.
LEWIS EVANS . I am a private in the Royal Marines. On the 10th of November I went to an alley at the back of Mr. Yewan's premises, and saw the prisoner come into the alley, stop there about a minute, and then break into Mr. Yewan's premises by getting through a hole in the railing—soon after I heard the rattling of a chain, and he crept out with the chain—I followed him to another street, where he offered it for sale, but the man would not buy it—I went and inquired for a constable, and while I went to the constable's house he threw the chain down—I ran after him, but lost him—this was on Saturday—I saw him again on Monday, working on the road—I did not know him before, but I am sure he is the person.
JUDITH CAMPION . I am the wife of William Campion, a constable. On Saturday, the 10th Nov., Mr. Evans came to my husband's shop-door, and asked for a constable—I heard a chain drop—I went out, picked it up, and took it into my house—Mr. Yewan afterwards claimed it.
WILLIAM CAMPION . I am a constable of Woolwich. I received the sling-chain from my wife—I took the prisoner on the Monday—he said he had offered it for sale.
( Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. Evans asked me to sell his great coat, and to sell the chain—he then said he would go and take me to the constable, unless I gave him 2 s. or 3 s., and I gave him 1 s.—he and another marine took money to let me go.
LEWIS EVANS re-examined. He never gave me any money—I never spoke to him at all till he was in custody at the station-house—I had no acquaintance with him at all—I tried to apprehend him, but he ran down a dark alley—there was another marine there, but he says he did not see him—I
heard him throw the chain down as J was at the constable's door—I did not stop him, not thinking it proper for me to take him—when he saw me speak to the constable's wife he threw the chain down.
GUILTY . Aged 18.*— Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18381126-223]
223. FRANCES COBARN was indicted for stealing, on the 17th of November, 1 watch, value 2 l.; 1 watch-guard, value 6 d.; 1 watch-chain, value 6 d.; 1 watch-key, value 1 d.; 1 seal, value 2 s.; 1 sovereign; and 6 shillings; the goods and monies of Charles Weightman, from his person.
CHARLES WEIGHTMAN . I am servant to Lieutenant Saville, of the Royal Artillery; I am a private. On the 16th of November my master sent me to Woolwich—I was to return next morning—I got up between four and five o'clock on the 17th, and went to the Coach and Horses public-house, to get a pint of beer and a glass of gin, about half-past five o'clock—the prisoner came in while I was there, with another young woman, and two men, dressed as sailors—they had something to drink—I got into conversation with the sailors—they asked me to drink at different times, and I became quite insensible by drinking with them—when I awoke I found myself is another public-house, the Duke on Horseback, in a room on the ground-floor—my watch, seal, and guard, a sovereign, and 5 s. or 6 s. were gone—this is the watch I lost—it is my property—( looking at it.)
MARY MARQUIS . I am single, and live in High-street, Woolwich. I went to the Coach and Horses on the morning in question—I saw the prosecutor there drunk—I remember the prisoner coming in with others—they drank together, and the prisoner left the house with the prosecutor, to go home together—I afterwards saw them at the Duke on Horseback—they had a quartern of rum there, and I partook of some there—the prosecutor produced a sovereign, and told me to pay for the rum—he laid it down, and before I could get hold of it, the prisoner took it up—she put it down at the bar to pay, and took the change herself—the prosecutor fell asleep there, and the prisoner tried to awake him—she took the guard off his neck, with the watch to it, and took it to the bar, to be taken care off till the man awoke—she was gone when he awoke—she did not give the prosecutor his change—he was in the parlour.
SUSAN FOX . I was at the Coach and Horses, and saw the prisoner come in, with another young woman and two men—she asked the prosecutor to drink—I afterwards went to the Duke on Horseback, and saw them there—they had more liquor there—Weightman threw a sovereign down on the table to pay for the rum—the prisoner took it up to the bar, changed it, and put the change in her glove—I then went out with her—she had a basket in her hand—I saw nothing of the watch—I was not noticing any thing at the time—she asked me to go down the street—I said I had no shawl on my neck—she gave me a shilling, which I gave her back—she bought me a shawl in the street, and asked me to go back—Mr. Campion came, and took the watch out of her hand at his own door as we passed—she must have got it away from the bar, but I had not seen her do it—she had money of her own in the basket—I do not know whether the shawl was paid for with her own money or the prosecutor's.
CHARLES HART . I am son to Mr. Hart, who keeps the Duke on Horseback—the prisoner brought me a watch, and told me to take care of it, saying it belonged to the man in the parlour—she came back afterwards,
and said the man was making a noise about it—I gave it to her, and she took it into the parlour, and then came out again—I found the prosecutor sleep in the room—the prisoner was also tipsy—15 d. was taken out of the sovereign for liquor.
WIILIAM CAMPION . I am a constable of Woolwich. On Saturday morning, the 17th of November, I went to find the prisoner, and met her, in company with two or three females, about half-past ten o'clock, about half a mile from The Duke on Horseback—I heard her say before I took her, that she should pull out her watch and see what o'clock it was—one of the women said, "You had better not, for fear you should break it"—I went up, and took it from her bosom—I found 10 s. 8 d. on her—I charged her with stealing the watch—she said, "Oh, let me see the man, I think I can make it right with him"—that she could make the sovereign up with the man, if she could see him—she had been drinking the knew what she was saying.
Prisoner's Defence. I left the young man asleep in the house—he threw the sovereign down on the table, and I paid for a quartern of rum at the bar—the young man asked me to take care of his watch and money for hint—I went out to take a walk, and took the watch to the bar for them to take care of—when I returned he was asleep—I could not awake him to give him the watch, and we went out as far as the Hampshire Hog to get some chops—in going flown the town, passing Mr. Campion's, I said, "We will go back to see if the young man is awake, and give him his watch"—Campion. then came out—they fetched the young man out, and he said he was very sorry, he had been asleep, and did not know what had passed.
WILLIAM CAMPION re-examined. She might have gone to The Duke on Horseback the way she was going, but it was very much out of her way from where she described she had been—I found no more money on her—after I took her she said she was going to take it back.
CHARLES HART re-examined. I returned her the watch almost directly after she brought it—I believe it was about nine o'clock—I did pot see her afterwards till she was apprehended—our waiter went in and awoke the prosecutor.
CHARLES WEIGH THAN re-examined. I was not aware the prisoner had got the watch, only from information the people in the house gave me—I was not told that Hart had got it—a man who cleaned up the room told me he believed a woman had gone out with it.
SUSAN FOX re-examined. The prisoner said something about going back to The Duke on Horseback, but Mr. Campion got up, and I could not hear what she said.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18381126-224]
224. SAMUEL BROOKS was indicted for stealing, on the 3rd of November, three shirts, value 6 s.; 1 jacket, value 4 s.; and 1 pair of breeches, value 5 s.; the goods of Lewis Davis, his master.
LEWIS DAVIS . I am a wholesale clothier, and live at Green's-end, Woolwich. The prisoner was four years in my service—I lost a quantity of articles, which I found in pledge—I employed a constable to search—( looking at a note)—the pawnbroker showed me this note—it is the prisoner's writing—when I supposed the prisoner was the thief I sent for him, and said to him, "I have missed a great quantity of these shirts; do you know any thing about it?"—he said, "No"—I said, "I am quite certain
there must be some unfair play here; have you had any one at the work-shop to-day who is likely to have taken any away?"—he said, "No"—I said, "What quantity of them have you*"—he said, "Not any"—I asked where he lived—he told me—I said, "I shall search, for I think you must know something of them"—he said he lived in Bray's-buildings—I said, "You have a lodging at Delagar's, the publican"—I called the office; and sent him with the prisoner to search the place.
WILLIAM THOMAS CHITTENDEN . I am a constable of Woolwich. On the 17th of November I went with Mr. Davis to the Earl of Chatham public-house, and was shown a room there—I found a bundle containing two shirts—the prisoner was sent for to—I opened the bundle, and took out the shirts—the prisoner produced the key of another box in the room—I found there another shirt, and detained him—I found on him a duplicate for a jacket and breeches.
THOMAS PACY BIRT . I am a pawnbroker, in Hare-street, Woolwich. I produce a jacket and breeches—I do not know who they were pawned by, but I gave the party this duplicate—this note was left with the things—I did not know the prisoner was constantly in the prosecutor's employ—I knew he was a tailor.
( Note read.)—"Sir, please to lend on this fisher boy's suit 6 s. " SAMUEL BROOKS."
LEWIS DAVIS re-examined. These articles are mine—they are not made for sale, but for the Navy.
Prisoner's Defence. I took them home to get a stain out of the one found in my box—the others I took home to get altered, which it was my duty to do—I had five suits of the clothes, and did pledge them, as I wanted money—I put the shirts on a chair when I took them home—the one stained was left with my wife to get the stain out, and I placed it in my box—the other two remained loose in the room for fire days—I did not secrete them—I was going to give them to a person to alter.
LEWIS DAVIS re-examined. These are navy shirts—it was the prisoner's duty to receive them when made, and send a cheque round for payment——those which are not made right he was empowered to get altered—not to take to his private lodging, but to give them to work-people who came to the shop—his wife worked for us.
GUILTY of stealing the jacket and breeches.—Aged 50.
Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18381126-225]
225. MARY LOVELL was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of November, 2 pairs of pattens, value 1 s. 10 d., the goods of James Gilham.
WILLIAM GILHAM . I am the son of James Gilham, a shoemaker in Nelson-street, Greenwich. On the 1st of November, while I was attending to a customer, I received information which induced me to go towards the hospital—I overtook the prisoner, and asked what she had in her basket—she made no reply—I saw through the top of the basket two pain of pattens—I told her she had stolen them from the door—she said she had bought them of a man in the market-place—they were my pattens, and had hung by the side of the doorway outside—I had seen them five minutes before.
MART POWELL . I am servant to Mr. Gilham. I was in the kitchen on the 1st of November, and saw the prisoner take the pattens off the hooks and put them into her basket—I told my master, he followed and took her.
DANIEL RIERDON ( police-constable R 207.) I went in search of the
prisoner, and found her in the park—I tool her to Mr. Gilham, who gave me two pairs of pattens.
( Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I bought them of a man in the market-place, for 6 d.
GUILTY . Aged 38.— Confined One Month.
[trialAccount t18381126-226]
226. EDWARD DONALD was indicted for stealing, on the 26th of October, 1 coat, value 10 s., and 1 pair of gloves, value 1 s., the goods of John Hop-wood.
JOHN HOPWOOD . I am porter to Mr. Taylor, an upholsterer in Dover-road. On the 26th of October, in the afternoon, I hung my coat across the horse in my master's van—I was taking a sofa into Mr. Holt's house, and when I came out my coat was gone off the horse—the gloves were in the pocket.
CHARLES NICHOLLS ( police-constable R 164.) On the 26th of October I was on duty about half-past six o'clock, and was informed that a coat was concealed under some leaves on the top of, Croom's-hill—I concealed myself in a garden, and about twenty minutes before eight o'clock the prisoner came in company with another one—he came direct to the spot, turned the leaves off, laid hold of the coat, and threw it over his arm—I jumped out and collared him, and the moment I collared him a pair of high shoes fell from him—I asked how he knew the coat was there—he said he did not know it was there, he saw it as he was passing, but he could not have seen it, it was quite covered with leaves—I took up the shoes—the other man made his escape—there was a donkey just by.
Prisoner. I was taken a quarter of an hour before the boots were produced—I know nothing of it whatever—I was walking across the park, there was a donkey before me and a young man—he said, "Look at that donkey"—I passed it, and it stumbled over the coat and turned the tail up—the constable came up and charged me with stealing it—he went there afterwards and found the boots.
Witness. The donkey did not disturb the leaves—I was concealed there an hour before he came—there was six inches of leaves over the coat, and it was three yards out of the path—the donkey did not tread near the leaves.
( Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY .—Aged 14.
[trialAccount t18381126-227]
227. EDWARD DONALD was again indicted for stealing, on the 26th of October, 2 pairs of shoes, value 16 s.; and 1 shoe, value 2 s.; the goods of William Newsom.
WILLIAM NEWSOM . I lost some shoes and boots out of my shop on the 26th of October, about half-past seven o'clock—my shop is about a quarter of a mile from Croom's-hill—I ran down the street on missing them, and went to the station-house—somebody must have got into my shop to get them—I was in the shop—they must have opened the door and crawled in.
CHARLES NICHOLLS ( police-constable R 164.) I saw the prisoner near the spot where the coat was concealed, about half-past seven o'clock or twenty minutes to eight—I jumped up and laid hold of him, and a pair of high shoes fell from him—I saw his companion go twenty or thirty yards from the spot, and go round the corner of a wall and stoop—I afterwards received information that these shoes were stolen—the prisoner denied that the shoes fell from him, but I am certain they did—I found the others behind the wall where I saw his companion stoop.
Prisoner's Defence. I know nothing of the slides—he took me ten minutes before he found them—he went and kicked up the leaves with his foot, and said I had dropped them.
Witness. No, I found them directly I laid hold of him—I kicked about to see if there was any thing else—I afterwards took a lantern, and found the rest where his companion had stooped.
( Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY .* Aged 14.— Transported for Seven Years to the Juvenile Prison.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.

229. RICHARD FOWLER and GEORGE HEATH were indicted for stealing, on the 1st of November, 1 watch, value 2 l. 10 s.; 2 seals, value 16 s.; 1 watch-key, value 3 s.; and 1 watch-chain, value 6 d.; the goods of Thomas Weaver, from his person.
THOMAS WEAVER . I live in Hogg-lane, Woolwich, and am a shoemaker. On the 1st of November I was at the Duke public-house, sitting by the fire with my gin-and-water—I got up, and some one took my place—I asked him to move—he struck me, and there was a row—the two prisoners were there—I lost my watch—I asked the landlord if he would lock the door—he said he would not—several more were sitting near me—Fowler was my second when I fought—he came and assisted me—I did not ask him to come—it was not with my consent—he held me round the waist, and I felt him feeling for my watch—Heath was in the room, but I did not see him do any thing—this is my watch—( looking at it.)
Fowler. Q. When you felt my hand at your watch, why did you not accuse me of it? A. I did not feel you take it—I went to the bar after it—I said I had lost my handkerchief, and afterwards found it in my pocket.
Fowler. Q. You went out and accused a female of taking your watch, and came back—I still remained at the bar, and had a pint of half-and-half—why did you not have me taken then?—bow can you say it was my hand against your watch, when there were from twenty-five to thirty people—did you see any marks on my hand? Witness. I did not see any marks.
Heath. Q. Can you tell how far I was off you? A. About two yards, I should think.
SARAH BUTCHER . I came out of the Duke public-house on that night—Heath came out, and went home with me—he said, "I have got something in my pocket that I gave 1 l. 1 s. for, you must pawn it tomorrow morning for 8 s., keep 2 s. for yourself, and give me the remainder—that was this key and seal—I went and gave them to the watchman.
Heath. Q. Did I ever say I bought it? A. Yes—I never saw you with any person—you came up to me when I was crossing the road.
CHARLES STEWART WARDEN . On the 2nd of November I went to the Earl Grey beer-shop, and I saw Fowler asleep—I left him, and from information
I went across the road, and there saw Conner, who is not in custody—I went to the Earl Grey again, aroused Fowler, and told him he was suspected of having stolen a watch—he said he had done no such thing, but because he was there he must be suspected—I found a half-crown and two shillings in his pocket—I then took him into custody with Conner, in the van to Woolwich—I found on Conner a duplicate of the watch—when they got off the van, Fowler squared up at me—Conner called out to Fowler to stick up to me, and Conner made his escape.
Fowler. Q. Did you not ask Conner whether it was his witch, and he said yes? A. I asked if it related to his property, and he said yes.
JOSEPH CUNNINGHAM . I am shopman to a pawnbroker at Deptford. Conner pawned this watch with me.
Fowler. Q. What time was it? A. Between half-past eight and nine o'clock—I did not see you there.
WILLIAM GREEN . I am a constable. Between one and two o'clock on the morning of Friday, Butcher offered me the key and seals—I went to her house and took the prisoner Heath, but previously when I was called into the house, I saw Fowler with his arms round the prosecutor, and Heath after that accused Butcher of taking the witch.
Fowler. Q. Did you hear that the watch was lost when you saw me in the house? A. No—I did not come into the parlour—the landlord had settled it himself—he was in before me—I did not see you afterwards—I had no business to shut the doors and search every person there—I came to quiet the row, and the landlord bad quieted it before I got to the door of the room.
Heath. You say that I ran away? Witness. No—I say you accused the girl of robbing the man of the watch—I took the girl away, and when I came back you were gone.
Fowler. He says he did not see me afterwards, and he states in the depositions that he saw me go through the gardens at half-past two o'clock in the morning with Conner. Witness. No—I saw Conner go through the garden at two o'clock in the morning, and another man, who, I believe, was Fowler—the row was between twelve and one o'clock.
Fowler's Defence. I went into this house about eleven o'clock, and Conner was there—I sat down, and had a pint of porter: lifter that Conner said, "We will have a pot between us"—we did to—I saw the prosecutor get up to get a glass of gin-and-water from the mantel-shelf—there, was a quarrel about a chair, and the prosecutor knocked a man down—I picked him up;—after that he went to the bar, and said he had lost his watch—the next morning I went to Greenwich and saw Conner come out of a public-house—we went on and went to another public-house—he said, "Stop there till I come back," which I did, and fell asleep till the officer came and apprehended me.
Heath's Defence. I was not in the house above half an hour, and I was awoke up—I picked up the seals in the tap-room, but I did not take them from the man's person.
JURY to THOMAS WEAVER. Q. Were the seals and key on the ring? A. Yes, and the ring was broken, I dare say—I have not seen it since—it was all together when I lost it—I have got one of the seals myself—the other seal and key the woman has produced.
NOT GUILTY
[trialAccount t18381126-230]
230. GEORGE WILSON was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of November, 8 yards of carpeting, value 30 s., the goods of Robert Lucas Jeeves and another.
ROBERT LUCAS JEEVES . I am in partnership with James Cobbett, a floor-cloth and carpet-manufacturer at Greenwich. On the 20th of November I was returning in my cart from Blackheath, and within three or four doors from my house, I saw a boy with this carpet on his shoulders—I suspected it was wrong from the pattern being reversed—in coming to my shop a boy touched his hat, and asked if I wanted an errand-boy—, but I would not be diverted from the carpet—I saw the boy who had it, give it to the prisoner about twenty yards off—I called to a person to take care of my horse and cart, and when I went round the way they went, I could see neither the carpet nor the prisoner, nor the boys—but in a tobacconist's shop I saw the prisoner—I wished to secure the boys—I saw them running towards Greenwich,—I thought I should lose the prisoner—I came back and he was gone from the shop—a witness came up and gave me information—this is the carpet—the prisoner had put it down it a tailor's shop.
JAMES SHEPHERD . I was looking out of my master's window—I saw a man come up with some carpet and place it at the door—the prisoner answers the description of the man, but I had such a slight glance of him that I cannot swear to him.
CHARLES PATNE . I live at Deptford-bridge. At half-past eleven o'clock, I was opposite the prosecutor's shop, and saw two boys—one first came out with this carpet on his shoulder—the other came out and met the prosecutor—he put his hand to his hat and said, "Do you want a shop-boy?"—I saw the boy that took the carpet give it to the prisoner who was waiting as if for the boys, and then I saw him with it.
Prisoner. Q. Did you lose sight of me? A. Yes, when you turned into the tobacco-shop—I saw you come out again—you had not the carpet then.
COURT. Q. Are you sure he is the person that had the carpet and took it from the boys? A. Yes, I did not see him put it down, but it was at a tailor's shop.
GUILTY . Aged 22.— Confined One Year.

231. WILLIAM HILL was Indicted for stealing, on the 16th of November, 2 pairs of clogs, value 2 s. 6 d., the goods of James Towers; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
EDWARD GEORGE SMITH . I have the management of Mr. James Towers's boot and shoe-warehouse, in London-street, Greenwich. About seven o'clock, on the 16th of November, the prisoner was brought to my shop, with two pairs of clogs, which I believe are my master's—I have missed two pairs—these are them—they have no mark on them.
JOHN EVANS ( police-constable R 190.) About seven o'clock that evening, I was on duty in London-street, and saw the prisoner walk from the prosecutor's shop—I stopped him and found this property under his arm—he was between six and eight yards from the shop when I took him.
THOMAS TAYLOR ( police-constable T 26.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction from Mr. Clark's office—( read)—the prisoner is the man that was tried and convicted.
Prisoner's Defence. I came out of the House of Correction in August—I found my character lost, and I was compelled to go and commit the crime over again—I could not get work—I came from Birmingham, and would return there again if I could—I have applied to the parish, and they would not relieve me.
GUILTY . Aged 46.— Confined Two Days.

232. ELIZABETH REYNOLDS was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of November, 14oz. weight of wax, value 6 d.; 1 watch-guard, value 2 s.; 2 pairs of scissors, value 4 s.; 1 purse, value 7 s.; 1 brash, value 9 d.; I pencil-case, value 4 d.; and 1 knife, value 2 d.; the goods of William Sulman, her master.
JANE SULMAN . I am the wife of William Sulman, a toy-dealer, in Stockwell-street, Greenwich. The prisoner was my servant—I went to her box and found some things, but nearly all were returned on the Thursday—here are two pairs of scissors, a brooch, a purse, a nail-brush, and flannel, which were in her box—she said her mother had given them to her—I asked her to fetch her mother—she said she could not—and then I told her to leave and send her mother for her wages.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. She returned to you and then left on account of illness? A. She was to come back when she got better—I believe she took them out of folly—she gave some of them to some persons—I was willing to take her back, but her father prosecuted her—I did not.
ELIZABETH READING being called, did not answer.
CHARLES NICHOLLS . I am a police-man. I went to the prisoner's father's house, and took her—I found in her pocket a knife, a purse, and thimble——the knife was identified by the prosecutrix—the prisoner's father sent me to take her.
NOT GUILTY .

233. RICHARD TERRY and JOSEPH TERRY were indicted for stealing, on the 7th of November, 4 decanters, value 1 l. 5 s. the goods of Joseph Burnby.
JOSEPH TERRY pleaded Guilty *—Aged 18.
JOSEPH BURNBY . I live at High-street, Deptford, and am a glass-dealer. At a quarter before twelve o'clock on the morning of the 7th of November, I was coming from the back premises and saw the two prisoners at the counter—I called to them and they immediately left the shop—I followed them—I missed four decanters—one was left on the board outside the door, and Joseph Terry dropped two of them on my following him.
BENJAMIN LOVELL . I am a police-man. I found this one decanter at the house of Joseph Terry, in about a quarter of an hour.
Joseph Terry. It was found at home, but I did not put it there nor take it.

November I lost this vice—( looking at it.)—the prisoner was a lodger at our house, but left two or three days after—I did not lend it to her—it was fixed in the wash-house.

ELIZABETH KIER . I am the wife of John Kier. I bought the vice of the prisoner for 5 1/2 d. as old iron.

235. WILLIAM BROWN was indicted for stealing, on the 26th of October, 1 pair of shoes, value 7 s., the goods of George Carpenter and another; and HENRY BURT , for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing it to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.
GEORGE CARPENTER . I am in partnership with Samuel Carpenter, a boot and shoemaker, in London-street, Greenwich. I was in the parlour on the 26th of October—I turned and saw Brown in the shop—I asked him what he wanted—he said he had done mischief, he had strewed some papers on the floor—he asked if I wanted any pattens or clogs—I said, no—as soon as he had left, I missed a pair of shoes—I went to the end of the street, and brought him back—I asked him if he knew any thing of the shoes—he said no, but that he saw a lad leave the shop with a bag on his back—I asked if he should know the lad—he said, yes—I took him to several places about the town, but could not find the lad—I let him go, but still followed him, and saw him go to the other prisoner—they went across the Creek bridge—each of them was carrying a bag—they saw me, and dropped their bags—I caught Brown and took him to Deptford—Burt's bag contained three pairs of shoes, one of which belonged to me—Burt was taken afterwards.
Brown. I had nothing at all in my Bag. Witness. Burt had not been in my shop that day—he could not have taken them.
Brown. I saw this boy—we went on towards London, and a man came and gave him the bag.
(Burt received a good character.)
BROWN— GUILTY . Aged 17.
BURT— GUILTY .
Aged 16.
Confined Three Months.
