237. HARRIET MANN was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of October, 1 bolster, value 4 s.; 1 pillow, value 2 s.; 1 cup, value 1 d.; 1 saucer value 1 d.; 1 pillow-case, value 6 d.; 1 knife, value 3 d.; 1 tumbler, value 1 s. 3 d.; and 1 printed book, value 3 d.; the goods of George Samuel Cole; and that she had been before convicted of felony.
GEORGE SAMUEL COLE . I am a fruiterer, and live in Trafalgar-road, Greenwich. The prisoner came to my house on the 9th of October, and hired a furnished room—she came in on the 11th and 12th, and was
there about four days, and four nights on and off—she went without notice—I then went into the room, and missed all these things—no other person had an opportunity of taking them—I have other lodgers who have been with me six months.
Prisoner. Other people had access to the room. Witness. Yes, they had.
GEORGE ROBINSON . I am a marine store dealer in the Old Woolwich-road. I produce a cup and saucer, a book and knife—I bought them of the prisoner on the 12th and 14th of October.
Prisoner. I asked you if you knew Mr. Cole, and you said yes—I sold them before I went to Mr. Cole. Witness. You asked me if I knew Mr. Cole, and I told you I did.
CHARLES REEVE . I am an apprentice to Mr. Stock, a pawnbroker. I have a sheet pawned on the 12th of October, but not by the prisoner—this is the duplicate of it.
ROBERT DUNN . I am a police-sergeant. I searched the prisoner's room, and found a corresponding duplicate to this shirt;, also a shirt and pillow pawned at Dawson's.
JOHN DAWSON . I am a pawnbroker. I have a sheet and pillow—this is the duplicate.
( Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. The room was open to any one—I had been out four days previous to the things being missed—the other things I sold before I went there—they were my own.
JAMES WILDS . I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction from Mr. Clark's office—( read.)—the prisoner is the person.
GUILTY . Aged 26.— Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18381126-238]
238. DAVID WHITE was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of October, 1 watch, value 2 l. 10 s.; 1 watch-key, value 2 d.; and 1 seal, value 2 d.; the goods of George Blackman.
GEORGE BLACKMAN . I am a bugleman in the first battalion of the rifle brigade. I had a watch and key safe on the 16th of October—I had it in my pocket at the Two Brewers at Maid stone, at a quarter before ten o'clock at night—I got drunk, but was sober enough to know that I had it then—I went to sleep at the Two Brewers—I awoke about eleven o'clock, my watch, purse, and other things were gone—my watch had been in my pocket—this is my watch and key—( looking at them.)
SAMUEL MARTIN . I am a sawyer, living in Union-street, Maidstone. I have a brother in the rifle brigade—I went into the Two Brewers that night—the prisoner and another came in—I know the prisoner—he was in there drinking—they were talking one to another—I asked him for a piece of tobacco—he gave it to me, and I fell asleep—when I awoke he was gone, and the prosecutor's watch was lost.
WILLIAM PHIPPS . I am a police-inspector. On the 18th of October I was on duty in Charlton fair—I took the prisoner and two others, and found on him this watch and key—I asked if it was his—he said yes, he had had it eight or ten years—that it was given him by his father, and he got it repaired at Woolwich, four or five months back.
GUILTY . Aged 28.

WILLIAM PHIPPS . I am a police inspector. I took the prisoner at Chalton Fair, and found four handkerchiefs on him—this was one, also a purse, a flash note for 5 l., a pair of scissors, a knife, tobacco-box, and key.

240. SAMUEL POLLARD was indicted for stealing, on the 3rd of November, 1 bag, value 1 d.; 1 half-crown, 6 shillings, and 1 sixpence; the goods and monies of John Tick.
JOHN TICK . I am a fishmonger, living at Greenwich. On the 3rd of November I saw the prisoner near my stall, in Beer-lane, Greenwich—I sent him on errands—he behaved with the greatest propriety from Friday till Saturday—on Sunday, when I came home to dinner my wife asked me if I had taken a bag out of the drawer containing 9 s.—I said no—I then sent for the prisoner—he came, and I accused him of it—he said he did not touch it—he said, "Why should I rob you, when you behaved so well to me?"—I then put it into the policeman's hands, and he asked him why he should rob this poor man, after he behaved so well to him—he asked him several questions, and then the prisoner got up, went to the balustrade of the square, and pulled out a purse containing 5 s., which was part of my money.
WILLIAM SUTTLE . I was present when the prisoner went for this money—he gave it into my hand.
GUILTY . Aged 16.— Confined Four Days.
(The prosecutor promised to take him into his employ again.)

241. JAMES RUSSELL was indicted for stealing, on the 25th of November, 1 pair of trowsers, value 5 s., the goods of William Oliver.
WILLIAM OLIVER . I am servant to James Cornish, of Plumstead. On Saturday night I purchased a pair of trowsers in the Kent-road—I put them into a sack, on the wagon which the prisoner was driving for me—he went on to Blackheath, and said, " Bill, your trowsers are lost, some one has taken them"—I said, "You must know something about it"—he denied it—I said there had been nobody in the wagon but our two selves—the policeman came up, and the prisoner began to cry—the officer pulled the trowsers out from under his waistcoat—the prisoner said, "You won't hurt me."
URIAH EDEN ( police-constable R 81.) I pulled the trowsers from under the prisoner's waistcoat, and took him in charge.
GUILTY . Aged 23.— Recommended to mercy by the Prosecutor.
Confined One Month.
Before Mr. Recorder.

242. JOHN HUNT was indicted for stealing, on the 9th of November, two dead fowls, value 4s., the goods of Erasmus Mawby; to which he pleaded

[trialAccount t18381126-243]
243. JAMES HOLT was indicted for stealing, on the 5th of November, 1 mare, price 26 l., the property of Thomas Hoath.
THOMAS HOATH . I am a market-gardener, and live at Merton, in Surrey. On the morning of the 5th of November I gave directions to Hughes, my man, to take my chesnut mare to a meadow belonging to Mr. Milton—I missed it the same evening—she had a short tail, and had a white mark on the face—she was about five years old—I saw her again nine days after I think, in possession of West, at Buntingford, in Hertfordshire.
Cross-examined by MR. BODKIN. Q. I believe she was placed in a field by the high-road? A. Yes—I believe the gate was left open by somebody.
CHARLES HUGHES . I put the mare in the field at Merton, on the 5th of November, and shut the gate when I left it—I went there again at five o'clock in the afternoon, and it was gone.
Cross-examined. Q. Had you fastened the gate, or locked it? A. I did not lock it—I did not see the mare that day afterwards—the gate does not open on the road—it is a side gate—there was nothing to prevent the animal coming into the road if the gate was open.
GEORGE CLOWSER . I am a bricklayer, and live at Merton. On the 5th of November, about three o'clock in the afternoon, I saw the mare running about at Merton, in the public read, going towards London—there was nobody with it—I saw it till it went out of sight—it was running—it was about a mile from the meadow it had been put in—the same road leads to the meadow—about five o'clock the same afternoon I saw her again—I know it was the same mare by a white streak on its face—it was a chesnut mare, and the one the prosecutor has claimed—I have seen it since—when I saw it at five o'clock it was coming in a direction from London, a very little way from the same spot, and the prisoner was running after it—I stopped it—the prisoner came and put a bridle on it, and led it away towards Wimbledon—he said that he knew the owner living at Wimbledon, and he would take her home—Wimbledon also leads towards where the mare was kept—Eyton was with me.
Cross-examined. Q. What is the prisoner? A. He lives with his father, and is a groom—he lives nearer to London than where I first saw the mare—it was running in a direction to pass his house—it was about two hours after that I saw her coming back, and he was following her with a bridle—he put the bridle on in my presence—I knew him and he knew me.
DAVID EYTON . I live at Merton. I saw the mare in the road about three o'clock, and took her to Mr. Griffiths's house, to ask if it was his—I afterwards turned her loose in the road—I was with Clowser two hours after, and saw the mare coming back, and the prisoner following it—he put the bridle on it—I knew him before—he was a groom, and was in Mr. Blake's service, but not at that time—his father is a maltster—I did not know that the mare belonged to the prosecutor.
HENRY HART . I am a constable. I apprehended the prisoner at Merton, at his father's house.
WILLIAM WEST . I am a constable of Mitcham. I found the mare at Buntingford, in possession of a person named Sydney, and delivered it to the prosecutor.
ALFRED RIX . I am bailiff to Captain Holland, Lord Holland's brother, at Molehurst, Hertford. On Thursday, the 8th of November, I went to Hertford fair, and saw the prisoner there—he said he had a chestnut-mare, and, as I knew the dealers better than himself, he wished I would sell it for him—I asked where he had got it—he said, "At Tattersal's"—the mare was swapped with George Clarke, of Stanstead, for a dark chestnut mare and eight sovereigns—I gave the sovereigns to the prisoner, and his boy took the dark chestnut mare to the Cross Keys for him—I have since seen the mare the prosecutor claims—it is the one the prisoner swapped for the other.
THOMAS HOATH re-examined. I had had the mare about a fortnight—I had not rode it about—it had never been out but once—very few people would know that it belonged to me.
NOT GUILTY .

245. DAVID PHILLIPS was indicted for stealing, on the 22nd of November, 1 pair of shoes, value 7 s.; the goods of Robert Williams Peacock.
THOMAS PEACOCK . I manage the business of my brother Robert Williams Peacock, a boot and shoemaker, in Tooley-street. On Thursday, the 22nd of November, a man came to the shop—I ran out in consequence of what he said, and met the prisoner, near Martin's, the pawnbroker's, in custody—Titchmarsh had the shoes—they were my brother's, and had hung on the door-post, in the street.
THOMAS LEWIS GREEN . I am seventeen years old, and am in the employ of my brother James, a fishmonger, in Bermondsey-street. On the 22nd of November I saw the prisoner, in company with a young man and a girl—the other young man looked at the shoes several times, and walked about the door with the prisoner, and tried to lift them off the hook—I told Titchmarsh, and he told Mr. Hall—after some time the other young man took them down, and handed them to the prisoner, who put them under his coat, and walked on—they were pursued, and the prisoner was secured with them, and taken into Martin's shop, where I saw the shoes fall down from him.
THOMAS TITCHMARSH . Green pointed out the prisoner, with a man and woman—I watched them for ten minutes, and saw them get the shoes nearly off the hook—I informed an officer, and went about my business—I
was returning, and Green called to me that they had got the shoes—I then stopped the prisoner, thrust him into Martin's shop, and the shoes fell at his feet—I held him till the officer came up.
SAMUEL JAMES HALL . I am a constable. I took charge of the prisoner and the shoes.
( Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY .* Aged 20.— Transported for Seven Years.
(The prisoner had been in custody more than thirty times.)
Before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.

[trialAccount t18381126-247]
247. THOMAS ELLIS was indicted for feloniously assaulting Richard Berry, on the 21st of August, at St. Mary Newington, putting him in fear, and taking from his person, and against his will, 1 watch, value 5 l.; 1 watch-guard, value 1 l.; 1 watch-chain, value 5 s.; and 1 watch-key, value 6 d.; his goods; and, at the time of the said robbery, striking, kicking and using other personal violence.
MR. RYLAND conducted the Prosecution.
RICHARD BERRY . I am a wagoner, in the employ of Mr. Coome, of Linfield, in Sussex. On the 21st of August I was with his wagon, going from London, home—I started from the White Hart, in the Borough—I got near the Elephant and Castle at half-past eleven o'clock at night, and had got into the wagon—the prisoner called me out of the wagon—I got out, and he begun to abuse me very much, and spit in my face twice, and asked me how I liked it—I said, "I do not like it much, but I suppose I must put up with it"—he then knocked me down—I had not said any thing to him—I felt I could not stand up against him, nor had I any disposition to do so—he knocked me down with his hand—whether he had any thing in it I cannot say—my mate was at the horses' heads driving the wagon—there were three men standing with the prisoner, very near him—they stepped back on the pavement as soon as I got out of the wagon—they remained there—I did not want to have any thing to do with it—I am fifty-seven years old—when I was knocked down I fell near the wheel—I was stunned by the fall—I understand the three men who were with him lifted me up into the wagon, but I was not in my senses—I came to my senses directly after, and found my pocket was turned inside out—I had had no money there—I found my watch and guard were gone—I had had the watch in my hand, just before, as I was late, and looked at it by Kennington-gate, against the time-piece, and it was exactly right—it was just by the Wagon and Horses, below the Elephant and Castle, that he called me out of the wagon—I had not sense enough to make an alarm, but when I came to myself I told my mate to stop, but we could not see an officer about, as it was Camberwell fair
time—the watch had been in my fob, and guard round my neck next my shirt—it cost 50 s.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. A. Was the prisoner a stranger to you? A. No, he has been for some years the Linfield carrier—I drive for Mr. Coombes, whose van is an opposition van to the prisoner's—he drives for his father—we never had any words before—his brother broke his leg in my master's employ, and they were angry with me because I took his place—I heard him swearing something about his brother before he struck me—I did not, on that, come out of my van, and ask what he meant by it—I will swear that—he called me out, and I asked what he wanted—he spat in my face, and began to call me all manner of names—I did not call him a s—y-nosed b—or a d—rogue, or any thing of the kind—I did not say any thing about his having been in gaol, or about his having been on one side of the wall, and I would try to get him on the other—I never lifted my hand against him—I applied to the Magistrate at Linfield for a warrant, and he gave it to me, and I got one from Union-hall—Mr. Bent did not refuse to give me one—the prisoner came down to Linfield with his wagon after this—I did not see him at the Quarter Sessions—I understood he was there—I went there, and they said it would be tried here—I never said I did not believe he took my watch—I said one of them must have taken it, I could not say which—I was quite sober.
Q. Did you ever say to any person that you never accused him of stealing your watch, but you were determined, by some means or other, to keep him off the road, the rest of his family would be of no use? A. No, never—I never said so to Peacock, a broker in the Blackfriars-road—I will swear that—Peacock came with Ellis, and wanted to make it up—I took him to be the gentleman, but he would not tell me his name—they sent for me over to a public-house—I told Peacock I could not think of making it up after being so dreadfully used—my watch was in my fob under my smock-frock—I had looked at it at the gate not three minute before—I stopped at Beckett's, and had a pint of beer among three of us—that was all I had—I did not stay there five minutes at the outside—Ellis was taking something up there—we generally call to see if there is any thing to take—he had his van there—we did not both stop there—he told my mate to stop, and said "Where is old Dick? I want to see him"—I do not know why he should spit in my face—I never had a mis —word from him in my life—I never raised my hand against him—I cannot say how long he has been in business—I have not lived at Linfield long myself—I lived at Brighton a good while—the last service I had was at the Catherine-wheel, in the Borough—I kept flys at Brixton, and kept a beer-shop in Kingsland-road, which I left because it did not answer.
MR. RYLAND. Q. How long had you known Ellis? A. Many years—I saw nobody but the three persons I have described, when I came out of the wagon, I thought one of them must have taken my watch—I am sure it did not drop—I never knew Peacock till he came with Ellis to make it up, and I refused, as I was bound over in 30 l. to appear.
JOHN GORRINGE . I am a wagoner. I was with Berry on the 21st of August—I went with the horses, and he with the wagon, and opposite the Wagon and Horses the prisoner came up, and asked me where my mate was—I said, "In the wagon"—he told me to stop the horses, which I did—he then called Berry out of the wagon—there were three more persons standing close to the prisoner at the time, I saw Berry come out of the
wagon—they all four stood together when he came out, and then the three stepped back on the pavement, and stopped there—Ellis kept calling Berry a blackguard, and every thing he could think of, and spit in his face twice—I did not hear Berry's answers, but he said something—I saw Ellis hit him with his doubled fist, and knock him down—he fell just before the front wheel of the wagon—I took hold of the horses' heads to prevent their over him—the three men stepped up from the pavement, took him ip, and put him into the wagon—I saw him about five minutes afterwards was getting better then, but was very poorly still, and complained of—before we had got to Newington-gate, he called to me, and told time—I knew he had a watch about him, and a guard round his the watch went into his fob—I had seen him with the watch in the afternoon—he said he had lost his watch when he complained of his head.
Cross-examined. Q. Were hard words used between Ellis and Berry? did they quarrel? A. Berry never said a word—he did not strike Ellis at all—there is a clock at Newington-gate, where he told me the time—I had seen his watch about three o'clock that afternoon—it was Camberwell fair time—the three persons were very officious helping him into the wagon—I saw Ellis speak to them—I do not know who they were—a man named Maynard just got hold of Berry, and said, "I will help you"—I never beard Ellis tell Maynard to help him in.
Q. Were you detected the other day in stealing some peas? A. Yes, I just took a handful from Mr. Podman's stack to eat—I did not think it any harm—Berry was with me, he was in the van.
MR. RYLAND. Q. Did you eat the peas? A. No, I threw them along the road—I ate one pod—I was not taken before a Magistrate for it—if they were worth a farthing, it was as much as they were—I saw Ellis speak to the three men before Berry got out of the wagon.
WILLIAM HONEY . I am ostler at the White Hart in the Borough. On the 21st of August, about eleven o'clock at night, Berry left with his van, and Gorringe with him—he was solid and sober then—I do not know whether he had a watch—I have heard him say he had, but I never saw him pull it out.
Cross-examined. Q. How far is the White Hart from Beckett's public-house? A. About forty rods—I never saw him tipsy since he went with the team—there were two horses to the van—Ellis's van puts up at our yard occasionally—sometimes his van has one hone, sometimes two, and sometimes more—he drives for his father, I believe—his brother assists him—he did drive for Combe—I do not know Maynard.
JOHN GORRINGE re-examined. I forgot to say, when Ellis knocked him down, that the people said, "Pull him away from the wheel," and Ellis said, "It will serve the old b—r right if the wheel ran over him."
Cross-examined. Q. Have you said that before? A. Yes—Berry did not tell me to come up and state this—I cannot say who stole the watch—I never said Ellis stole it, and I never said he did not steal it—nor that I did not think he stole it.
MR. PAYNE called
THOMAS MAYNARD . I am the prisoner's mate—he lives at Linfield, and drives his father's van. I remember the van stopping at the Waggon and Horses on the night in question—Ellis's van was there first, and Berry's van came up—Ellis said, "Where is Dick Berry?"—the boy stopped the horses, and said, "He is up in the van"—Ellis said, "And the
best place for him"—Berry hallooed out, "What do'you mean by that?"—he said, "Why, what do you mean about my brother Bob and about me?"—Berry then got out of the wagon and called Ellis a rogue, a s—y nosed rip, and a liar, and struck him—Ellis then struck him again, and knocked him down—Berry was very drunk—I assisted in pulling him into the van by desire of a gentleman who stood by—there was nobody with Ellis besides me—we had come from the Talbot, which is about a mile from the Coach and Horses—we had only been there a very few minutes before Berry's van came up—I never saw Ellis spit in Berry's face at all—we stopped at Beckett's a minute or two.
MR. RYLAND. Q. What time did you leave the Talbot? A. About ten o'clock—we went direct from there to the Coach and Horses, and got there about half past ten o'clock—we stopped there about ten minutes, and then went on our way—I did not see three men standing talking to Ellis—there were no men talking to him at all—some came afterwards—if there had been three men talking to him, I must have seen them—an old gentleman helped him into the wagon—I did not see three persons help him into the wagon—I helped him, and must have seen them if there had been—I will swear they did not—I do not think Berry was much hurt—he was very drunk—he appeared to be so when he came out of the wagon—he reeled—he could hardly stand—he was very abusive, and talked a good deal—it was not a dark night—any body could see three men if they were there—the old man had a brownish coat, dark trowsers, and a black hat—it was a clear night—he had a stick in his hand—he had not much difficulty in putting the prosecutor into the wagon—he put him on the shaft, and he crawled in.
JONATHAN PEACOCK . I am a commission-traveller, and live at No. 8, Nelson-place, Nelson-square, Blackfriars-road. On the night in question I was at Mr. Jones's, who keeps the Coach and Horses, and about ten or eleven o'clock I saw the two vans there—as I came out, I heard the persons standing about, say to Ellis, "Why don't you strike him again, he has struck you first, and abused you grossly?"—Berry then came, and attempted to strike him again, and Ellis, to defend himself, struck him and knocked him down—I imagined it was a mere drunken quarrel between the two carriers—I considered they were both drunk—I did not help Berry into the van—I saw an elderly person in a dark dress, who I do not know, do so—I did not see three men step back on the pavement—there were ten or twelve persons about when I came out, but I was not there at the first of it—the prisoner afterwards called on me, and I went with him to the prosecutor to see if the matter could not be arranged without any further delay or expense, as I was daily expecting to go down to Hull on business of importance—we went to a house in the Borough-market, and sent for the prosecutor—he came, and I said it was a very unfortunate thing, could not the matter be settled—he said he did not care much about it, only he had lost his watch, which I was not aware of—I said, "Do you accuse Ellis of stealing it?"—he said, "No, I do not accuse him, but it was through him I lost it; I can't do any thing in it, you must consult some other party, for we are determined to drive him off the road, and then the rest of his family will be of no use"—that was in Ellis's hearing—finding that to be his determination, the matter ended—he took a glass or two of ale with me, and went about his business.
MR. RYLAND. Q. For whom are you engaged as a commission-traveller
A. Messrs. Molt, of Whitechapel, soap-manufacturers—I have 6 d. per hundred for selling—I have travelled for them nine years in town—I have no salary, but a commission—I deal with grocers and oilmen in soap and spices—I have sold to Messrs. Green, of Leather-lane, oilmen, within these six months, to the amount of 200 l. or 300 l. perhaps, and with Messrs. Lloyd, of City-road, grocers, within these three months, to the amount of 40 l. in soap—and a great many others, on account of Messrs. Molt—I have lodged in Nelson-place for eight years—I was formerly in a very extensive way of business as a grocer, in Black friars-road, facing Rowland Hill's chapel, where I lost some thousands—I was a bankrupt in 1825—I deal in spices on my own account—I have no place of business—I went to the Coach and Horses with a foreigner to take a glass of ale—Mr. Jones, whose father keeps the house, has been a friend and customer of mine many years—I think it was between ten and eleven o'clock I was there—I frequently call there as I pass by—I saw both parties distinctly when I came out—I considered them both drunk—an elderly man and Ellis's boy helped Berry on to the fore-shafts—I did not see him put into the wagon—I don't think Ellis was so drunk as Berry—I have known Ellis six or seven months—I am not in the habit of calling him Tom —I never heard that he and Berry were rivals in business—I tried to settle the matter, to save the trouble and expense of going down to Lewes—I had not heard then that Ellis was charged with stealing the watch—Berry did not say there were three other men with Ellis when he lost it—he did not describe how he lost it—I cannot say whether the twelve persons I saw belonged to the "Swell Mob"—it was rather a dull night—I did not say it was a clear night—there was plenty of light from Jones's window, but it was a foggy night, rather, not decidedly foggy—you might discern a dozen yards before you—I think, when it was all over, it could not have exceeded half past ten o'clock.
GUILTY of an assault. Aged.— Confined Four Months.
Before Mr. Baron Alderson.
[trialAccount t18381126-248]
248. JOHN HARRIES was indicted for feloniously forging and uttering, on the 28th of June, an acceptance to a bill of exchange for 100 l., with intent to defraud Francis Thomas Biddulph and others.
MR. PHILLIPS conducted the Prosecution.
DAVID HARRIES JAMSS . I live at Llandilovawr, Carmarthenshire. I am a Magistrate for Pembroke—the prisoner is my brother-in-law—my sister is now dead—I had assisted him with money to a considerable extent before her death—I ultimately found that he was committed to Carmarthen gaol for debt—( looking at a bill.)—the acceptance on this is not my hand-writing—I did not authorise the prisoner to sign it for me—I found that it had gone to Biddulph's bank—I went to Carmarthen prison to see the prisoner—I said, "I find you have forged my name, and you must abide by the consequences, for I will never pay one farthing of the money"—he made no reply—there was no one with me at that time—I left him—that was about the 8th or 10th of September, 1887—I went and saw him again five or six days afterwards, with Mr. Lewis Morris, the prisoner's attorney, but said nothing to him then—this acceptance bears a resemblance to my signature—the body of the bill is the prisoner's handwriting.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. When asked if the prisoner was
your brother-in-law, you answered, your sister was dead? A. Yes, I do not consider him a brother-in-law any longer—there is still a tie by the marriage—he is a first cousin of mine besides—he is the son of a broths of my mother's—his wife died in October, 1837.
Q. Have you been in the habit of accepting bills and procuring discounts for each other? A. I have for him, and he has for me, up to about the year 1831—that ended in 1831—I have been in the habit of giving him blank accommodation acceptances—I think I did so in 1831, before Walter's bank failed at Carmarthen—have not done so since, to the best of my knowledge—I have certainly not done so in the last year, in no part of it—I have not done so in 1836, to the best of my knowledge—I may have done so and forgotten it—I have not such a recollection as enables me to say I did not do it—I have been compelled to prosecute the prisoner in consequence of the loss of my sister, and other circumstances—Mr. Biddulph has nothing to do with the prosecution—I do not know of a person named Phillips having brought an action against the prisoner in the Borough Court of Carmarthen—I do not know that he had the prisoner in prison for debt—I gave 20 l. to get a habeas to bring the prisoner to London—he has been in custody on civil process since August last year—he had been sued in the Borough Court of Carmarthen before I did that—I gave the 20 l. about two months ago, long after I knew of this forgery—all his friends and relations live in Carmarthenshire—I never authorised my attorney to indemnify Phillips against the coats in bringing him to London—I never told bun to bear Phillips harmless in the expense of bringing the prisoner to London, or in his costs—I preferred this hill is October, thirteen months after I found out the offence—an action had been brought against me about the bills in the mean time, and a verdict passed against me—I have preferred two indictments—one it for a 200 l. bill—that is the one on which the verdict was obtained against me, and on which I have obtained a new trial, on payment of costs, since this indictment—the prisoner was examined as a witness on the trial in August last—I heard him swear that I gave him authority to put my name to both bills—the trial was in Carmarthen.
Q. After that trial on which a verdict passed against you in Carmarthen, you paid for a habeas to remove him to London, and being in custody on civil process, have you indicted him for this offence? A. I did—he has lived twenty years in Carmarthen, and carried on business in a large way—I have not obtained any property from him—he gave me a nominal security—I will show it if you wish it—I do consider it nominal, as it is worth nothing—it is a mortgage, but there was a prior mortgage, which exists to the full value—I had it given me on the 8th of June, 1837—I did not know of the prior mortgage at that time—Mr. Biddulph claims it—his clerk told me so yesterday—the mortgage was for money I became security for before—I know that a fiat of bankruptcy had been issued against the prisoner—this deed recites three prior mortgages for 1600 l., but there is another which I did not know of—there is 1300 l. cannot be sold—he has only a life interest in it—I cannot tell the value of it—I entered into a bond of 600 l. for him, to a person named Moss—I gave a note of hand for 300 l. to a person, which I paid—this mortgage was given as a general security for all debts I might have to pay for him—he owes me money now—there is a time limited in the deed—I have not sold the estate, as there is a prior mortgage—I had become security at the time he executed this—1000 l. is the amount he is charged by that mortgage—at that time I had not advanced
more than 900 l., but I was security for more—there was a bond for 600 l., a note of hand for 300 l. at Jones's, and a bill of 82 l. at Wilkin's bank—it was an accommodation bill—I never had a farthing of the interest paid me on this mortgage—it was about 1829 I was security for the 600.—I know the bond was before 1830—I found he was in difficulties, and got the security to indemnify myself if I could—I went to the prisoner's office on that occasion—I did not tell him if he would give me the security to enable at to cover the 600 l. and 800 l., I would do any thing for him I could—he did not tell me he should want the use of my name for 500 l. or 600 l.—I cold him he must pay the securities off, for I would not be responsible for soy money for him—I make ft mistake—I meant to say I would not be responsible any longer for the amount which I was security for—that he must pay them—what I meant to do was to go to the parties, and ask them to call the money in—I was not a creditor at that time, only a security—I lever knew that he had the signatures of three-fifths of his creditors to his certificate—I heard he was to be discharged from prison in September, 1887—he told me so when I saw him in prison—I heard it rumoured in; Carmarthenshire that he was likely to be discharged while he was in London—it was not on that that I came up and indicted him—on my oath, I did not come up with in a week of the time I beard it—I heard it on my way up from Carmarthen—I live twenty-five miles below Carmarthen—I was coming up to get these indictments preferred against him—it was some time in October, not before, about thirteen months after I had seen him in prison—I went to the different holders of the bills, to declare them as forgeries—I went first to him in prison, to know if the bills were still in existence which he had forged—I knew of the bills by the holders applying for payment—the first application was in September, 1837—that Was not about the 900 l. bill on which the action was brought—the bill was not due at the time I went to him in prison, but I was told there were bills in existence with my signature, which I was not aware of—it was neither of these bills I was applied to for payment of—it was a third bill—that was at Carmarthen—I have kept some cases back—William Phillips holds one, and Posthumous Phillips the other—I am not aware that they are any relations to the man I gave the 20 l. to go to get the habeas —Post-humous Phillips is a maltster—the bill be has is for 150 l.—I have been called on to pay it—I have not paid any part of it—I have taken no steps on it—the other is for 100 l.—William Phillips is the holder of that—those bills are signed by procuration—I do not know what drawing by procuration is—I have never seen those bills—I was called on to pay them twelve months ago.
COURT. Q. How do you know that they are not your signature? A. I know I never signed such bills.
MR. CLARKSOK. Q. Has not your wife stated to you repeatedly that you had put your name to so many accommodation bills that you did not know the number? A. My wife was very much annoyed when she found it out, but I do not know that she ever used such words as those—I will not swear she did not—I do not know what answer I made her—my wife sometimes writes letters for me—an application was made to me by Mr. Jones for payment of the 200 l. bill I have indicted the prisoner for—my wife wrote a litter in answer to Mr. Jones, by my direction, in November, 1837—Morris and Son were the holders of the bill—the action was brought after I had been to Carmarthen to see Jones—I went to declare them forgeries—I did not request Lewis Morris to go with me to the prisoner in gaol—he was not with me when I declared the forgery—the only
time he went with me to the prison was in September—that was not the first time I went—I asked him to go with me—there was no statement of the prisoner's affairs and accounts gone into then—the balance-sheet was not made—Morris did not ask the prisoner to explain an item of 400 l. stated to be due to Mr. James—he did not ask who the Mr. James was to whom the debt of 400 l. was stated—I will swear he did not ask it in my hearing—the prisoner did not reply that it was Mr. James, his brother-in-law, now present—Mr. Morris did not inquire of what the 400 l. was made up, nor did the prisoner state it was 200 l.—a bill and note for 100 l. due to William Phillips, and another for 100 l. due to Biddulph, nor any words to that effect—nor did he state, "In all of these Mr. James is liable as the acceptor"—I have seen his balance-sheet—it was about the latter end of September, or beginning of October, that the prisoner was brought from Carmarthen—Morgan Rice James is an attorney—he has been my attorney, he is not now—I sent him to Carmarthen gaol about this, in September, 1837, as I found there were more forgeries—I was applied to for another bill, which I knew nothing of, which was forged—that was the 200 l. bill of Morris I have indicted him for—I went to Haverford West, and spoke to my professional man, and asked him to go up and inquire whether that 200 l. was the extent of the forgeries—I had heard of two before that, held by William and Posthumous Phillips—I had seen him twice in prison.
Q. Why have you not preferred charges for those bills? A. I did not care which, I left it to my professional man to choose—my present attorney lives in London—I did not direct the two bills of Phillips's to be brought to London.
Q. Was not the bill on Morris, which induced you to send Mr. M. R. James to the prisoner in gaol, due in October? A. That was for another 100 l., which I never heard any more of.
Q. Then there seems to have been thousands according to your statement? A. I cannot say—I did not send Mr. M. R. James to see if he could get any admission from the prisoner—I sent him to know the extent of the forgeries—he was examined as a witness for me on the trial last summer, and he swore, in my presence, that the prisoner admitted the forgery, and the Jury found for the plaintiff.
Q. Pray have you got any security besides that you have produced, either from the prisoner, or his family? A. I have security from his mother—I got that, I think, at the latter end of 1837—I have not brought it to London—I received it, I think, in October, after I sent Mr. M. R. James to the prison—it is a mortgage—it does not state any amount—I took it after I found there was another mortgage, I mean Biddulph's mortgage—I do not know the amount of that—I have heard it is 600 l., but Mr. Hulme, the clerk, said yesterday he thought it would cover the other 100 l. also—I know his mother's property lets for 55 l. a-year—I have a general charge, without any amount stated—there is no incumbrance on it—she gave me that as security that I should not he injured by her son—I think Mr. Gwynne was the prisoner's detaining creditor—I did not know he was going to arrest him—he was examined on the trial as a witness—Mr. Hulme was not—the case was tried before Mr. Baron Gurney.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Before you went to the prison with Mr. Morris, had he requested of you to avoid any particular subject? A. Yes, he had—I was the defendant in the 200 l. action—the prisoner was a witness.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Can you give any idea of the extent of accommodation-paper
which has passed between you and the prisoner? A. No, I Cannot—I can within hundreds.
COURT. Q. You say the security was given for liabilities previously incurred by you? A. Yes, the mortgage—I was not to do any thing for him at all—if I was called on to pay the securities, that was to indemnify me, I was advised so to do by my attorney.
WILLIAM HULME . I am clerk to Biddulph and Co., of Carmarthen. I received this bill, to the best of my recollection, from the managing partner—I afterwards saw the prisoner about it—perhaps the day or day after it was due and dishonoured—I showed him the bill, and told him it was dishonoured—I do not recollect what he said to it—it is a single bill, and among the many thousands passing through my hands, it is not likely I should recollect—I do not know that he said any thing when I told him it was dishonoured—I had known him eight or ten years—the bill was due on the 17th of September, 1837—it bears his indorsement—to the best of my belief it is his own hand-writing—I have seen him write frequently, and believe it to be his—he has indorsed it to Messrs. Biddulph—I have heard it pronounced a forgery—I made a demand, both on the drawer and acceptor, when it became due—we gave notice of the bill being dishonoured, which the prosecutor never attended to—he called some few months afterwards, and requested I would show him the bill, which I did—I spoke to the prisoner about it, and he told me it was not a forgery—I told him the prosecutor had said the bill was a forgery—he replied, there was a conspiracy, he believed, got up against him, but it was no forgery—that there was an attempt to transport him, or something of that sort—I did not call his attention to the signature of the acceptor, and he said nothing about the acceptance—I do not know who brought the bill to us—it was passed to the credit of the prisoner's account—the prisoner, I believe, did not bring it to me.
MORGAN RICE JAMES . I am an attorney, living at Haverford-west. Last September twelvemonth I went to Carmarthen prison, to see the prisoner—I asked him if there was not a bill at Biddulph's bank, purporting to be signed by Mr. James—he said there was—I asked him whether there were any other bills—he said there were—he told me there was a bill of 200 l. at Messrs. Morris, the banker's, and one of 100 l. at Mr. William Phillips's, and another, I think, for 100 l. at Mr. Posthumous Phillips's—I asked him whether he had any authority from Mr. James to sign those bills'—he said no, he had not—I saw him twice or three times the same day—I recommended him to make out his accounts, and get the business settled, as it was a serious case, and he promised me he would—I told him I was sent to him from Haverford-west, to inquire what bills he had forged, and he then told me the bills he had forged, as I have already mentioned—I do not know whether that was the way it occurred—I was with him some time, and on friendly terms with him—I did not know I should have to state what transpired—I have related the substance of what passed—I do not know whose writing the acceptance to this bill is—I have seen the prosecutor write repeatedly—I do not believe it to be his writing—I should say it is but a humble imitation of it.
Cross-examined. Q. You mean by that, it is a very bad imitation? A. It is not very good—I have seen a better one—I am a distant relation to the prosecutor, and bear the same relationship to the prisoner—I only came because called on to come by the prosecutor—I am very sorry to be here—the prisoner had been confined for debt about a month when I
had the interview with him—I did not take any bills of exchange with me—I did not produce any to him—I have heard that the prisoner has done a large business at Carmarthen, as a wine and spirit merchant, but I live twenty-two miles from him—I think the prosecutor called on met to go to him on Saturday, the 16th of September, and I proceeded the following day.
Q. I suppose, from the situation you filled between the parties, whatever passed was friendly coversation, and in confidence with the prisoner? A. No—my client, Mr. James, seemed alarmed—he is not my client now—I did not know the prisoner was in London till he came—I was examined as a witness at Carmarthen—I do not know whether there has been a sale of the prisoner's goods—I never asked him for the balance of what the goods produced, to cover Mr. James.
WILLIAM HULME re-examined. Messrs. Biddulph sent this bill to the Bank of England shortly after it was drawn—it would be in the Bank of England in September, 1837—we applied to Mr. James for it on the 20th of 21st of September, for the first time, it was due on the 17th of September—I am positive we did not apply before it became due—the prisoner has carried on a very large business as long as I have known him, and bore the character of an honest man.
THOMAS GWYKNE . I am an attorney, living at Haverford-west In November, 1837, I went with Mr. James to Carmarthen prison, to see the prisoner—Mr. James said to him, "John, are you prepared to pay these crooked bills?"—the prisoner said he was not—Mr. James then said, "You must take the consequences"—allow me to explain that I was going to the gaol on my own business, to see Mr. Harris, and Mr. James asked me to accompany him—this occurred just as we left—a great deal passed before, but nothing more about the bills.
MR. CLARKSON to MR. D. H. JAMES. Q. Were the prisoner's goods sold in September? A. There were two sales—I think the sale of his furniture was about November—I do not think it was before I went to the prison, but I will not swear it—I swear it was not a month before I went to the prison—I cannot say whether the stock of the farm was sold in September—I do not think he was made a bankrupt till the spring of 1838—I do not know who struck the docket—I have not proved any debt under the commission—there was an application made for the balance of his goods in the hands of the Sheriff, but it was not made on my own account—I did it for him to come out of prison—I did not get it—I believe it was from 100 l. to 200 l.
NOT GUILTY .
(There was another indictment against the prisoner, upon which no evidence was offered.)
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.

250. EDWARD WILLOUGHBY was indicted for stealing, on the 7th of November, 2 pairs of stockings, value 6 d.; 1 night-cap, value 4 d.; 1 handkerchief, value 6 d.; 2 pinafores, value 9 d.; and 1 pair of drawers, value 2 s.; the goods of Henry Phillpot: and 1 gown, value 1 s.; and 2 aprons, value 6 d.; the goods of Mary Duell.
DESIMA PHILPOTT . I am the wife of Henry Phillpot, of Battersea. On the 7th of November, my servant hung these things in the yard to dry—I saw them out about five o'clock in the evening, and they were missed about eleven o'clock—these are my things, and Mrs. Duell's—( examining them)—the prisoner lodged with us for a night or two.
WILLIAM REED ( police-sergeant V 18.) I heard of these things being lost, and I went after the prisoner—I found him about six o'clock the next morning with them.
Prisoner's Defence. I found them tied up in a bundle.
GUILTY . Aged 24.— Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18381126-251]
251. GEORGE STEVENS was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of I November, 2 reflectors, value 2 s.; 2 lamp-irons, value 2 s.; and 2 lamps, value 10 s.; the goods of Frederick Dawson, his master.
FREDERICK DAWSON . I am a carriage-manufacturer, in Long-acre. The prisoner worked for me about four months, and left on Tuesday—I missed things, and had suspected every one in the place, and last Sunday week my apprentice informed me that two lamps of mine were in a shop at the corner of William-street, Cornwall-road—I went there, and saw these lamps, with these reflectors in them—they were made for another pair of lamps, but the prisoner had fitted them up for sale—the prisoner lodged in that house.
Prisoner. Q. Can you swear to these lamps? A. Yes.
Prisoner. I can prove by five or six people that they taw me make them in Lambeth, and these reflectors too.
GEORGE HARRISON . I am an apprentice to the prosecutor. I saw these: lamps at the corner of Cornwall-road, in William-street—I firmly believe them to be my master's, and the cones inside I can swear to from their being blocked, which all lamps are not—I missed them, and could not find them.
Prisoner. There are hundreds of pairs of cones made on the same principle as these—I only finished them the week before.
JOHN LAMBERT ( police-constable L 29.) The lamps were brought to the station-house, and I took the prisoner—he said the lamps were his, and he made them.
( Witnesses for the Defence.)
THOMAS ANKERS . I live at Nos. 15 and 16, Windmill-row, New-cut, and am a tin-man. These lamps, or some like them, were made in my place, three weeks ago last Sunday, by the prisoner—he does not lodge with me, but he asked my permission to make a pair of lamps there—I said he might do it—he came two or three evenings before, and on Sunday three weeks he came and finished them at my place—I went in a little before dinner, and saw him at work with lamps similar to these—I dare say they are them—they were all to pieces and bits on the bench—this come I had in my hand, or one I could almost swear was the same, and one of these lamps I can swear to—it was bruised in the nozzle in a bad way—this is the one—( looking at it.)—I have never had the lamps in my hand since they were made till this moment—I said, "You have made a botch of this and spoiled it"—"No," says he, "it won't matter"—this was three week last Sunday.
HANNAH HAYES . I am a widow. I saw Stevens making these lamp last Sunday week—I live with Thomas Ankers—he is my brother-in-law—I am sure it was last Sunday week—I saw him soldering them from a little charcoal—he was finishing them—my brother-in-law was not with me, but he was at home that day—he was out and in all day long, and had every opportunity of seeing them—I am quite sure it was last Sunday week.
JOHN LOVELER . The prisoner was a lodger of mine—he told new Sunday that he was going to make a pair of lamps—he brought them home in the evening—it was last Sunday three weeks, about six o'clock in the evening.
FREDERICK DAWSON re-examined. I lost the cones about six weeks ago.
GUILTY . Aged 55.— Confined Three Months.

253. AGNES WALKINSHAW was indicted for stealing, on the 3rd of November, 1 watch, value 3 l.; 1 seal, value 10 s.; and 1 chain, value 1 s., the goods of John Brown Sims, from his person.
JOHN BROWN SIMS . I live in Short-street, Horsleydown, and am a lighterman. On Saturday night, the 3rd of November, as I went into the Union, in Tooley-street, the prisoner came in and asked me to give her a glass—I went home with her to Unicorn-square—I had my watch when I went there—when I was coming away I missed it, and asked her to give it to me—she said she would give it me for half-a-crown—the woman of the house came up and called the policeman—the prisoner then said she never saw it—he searched her as far as decency would allow, and it was not found, but in going to the station-house it was dropped—this is it—I had paid her one shilling.
Prisoner. You said you would give me half-a-crown, and then you gave me the watch—I said I would give it you for half-a-crown—you said you did not know whether you had given it to me or not—it is an old spite he has had against me.
JOHN KELLY ( police-constable M 85.) I was called—the prosecutor said, this female had got the watch—she said she had not got it—I then searched her as far as decency would allow, but could not find it, and on the way to the station-house I saw it drop—the prosecutor was quite sober, and the prisoner also, as far as I could judge.
GUILTY . Aged 20.— Recommended to mercy.— Confined Three Months.

JAMES WILLIAM BURRIDGE ( police-constable V 160.) On the 3rd of November I was in the counting-house, and saw him coming along—I went and took him by the collar into the counting-house, and he took some of this wool out of his breast—he was then taken—part of this was found about him, and this was on his person—( producing it.)
DANIEL JOSEPH HURLEY . I am in the employ of Mr. Brown—I saw part of this taken from the prisoner's person.
Prisoner. It was lying outside the gate, and I picked it up.
JOHN THOMAS CAMPBELL re-examined. No, it was in a bale—I examined the bales, and found one was cut—it was the same sort of wool as this.

256. BRIDGET TOOMEY was indicted for stealing, on the 28th of October, 1 crown-piece, the monies of Thomas Bryant.
THOMAS BRYANT . I am in the employ of Mr. Sherwood on the railI went into a shop in Southwark to buy some muffins, and put down a crown-piece—the man had no change—the prisoner followed me in, and wanted me to give her a muffin—she snatched up the five shilling piece, and said she would go and get change—I ran after her, and caught her—I asked her to give it me—she would not—I held her till the people came up.
Prisoner. Did you not drag me into the shop, and say you would give me a muffin? Witness. No.
HENRY SMITH . I am an apprentice to John Green, in Kent-street, a shoemaker. I saw the prosecutor go into the muffin shop in King-street—he put the money down on the counter—she took it up and ran out—the man said, "You go after it, or you will lose it"—he did, and she said she would not give it him.—he went to get a policeman—I and another young man watched the prisoner, and she went up some stairs—the money has not been found.
Prisoner. The prosecutor said he had been robbed by two girls before he met me, and then he asked me where they were—I did not rob him.
GUILTY .* Aged 21.— Transported for Seven Years.

257. WILLIAM DADY and JOHN ROBINSON were indicted for stealing, on the 7th of November, 12 glass bottles, value 1 s.; 2 jugs, value 1 d.; and 1lb. weight of grease, value 2 d.; the goods of Jeremiah Michael Evans, the master of Dady.
JEREMIAH MICHAEL EVANS . I live in Clapham-rise—Dady was in my employ as footman. I missed some property, and charged Dady with having stolen certain empty bottles—he acknowledged his guilt, and begged for forgiveness, it being his first offence—I said it was my duty to give him in charge—I procured a search warrant, and went with the officer—I found at Robinson's house a great quantity of bottles, among which were the two which I now produce.
ANN WENDON . I am housemaid to the prosecutor. On Wednesday, the 7th of October, I saw Dady take some glass bottles out of the stable and deliver them to Robinson, who was in the yard on my master's permises—I think he took about a dozen—he put them into a hamper, took them out of the yard, placed them on a barrow, and wheeled it away—I asked Robinson where he got them from—he said he brought them from Balham-hill—he came back in about ten minutes, and then he had a bundle and a pitcher with kitchen grease in it—it came from Mr. Evans's house—I asked him what business he had there—he said he had nothing more than what he paid for.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. How many bottles did you first see in the hamper? A. Two or three—I never issued invitations to fourteen persons to come to my master's house—I never had but one person to see me—three people called to see the cook one afternoon—they staid a very short time—I do not remember that there was a party there on Sunday, the 4th of November—Mary Barton, the late servant, called on Sunday, and my master was in town—there were two broken jugs, an old cullander, and lib. of grease, value 3 d. missing.
JAMES GRACE ( police-constable V 24.) I took the prisoner in charge.
Cross-examined. Q. Produce the two broken jugs and the cullander, A. Here they are—Robinson lives not above three or four hundred yards off—I found the jugs in the yard adjoining his house, where there were a good many things kept—the cullander was in a yard, thrown out in a heap all lying together with other such property.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18381126-258]
258. THOMAS ELDERSHAW was indicted for stealing, on the 29th of October, 1 gelding, price 4 l., the property of Joseph Wilkinson, his master.
JOSEPH WILKINSOW . I reside at No. 265, Kent-street, Surrey, in the parish of St. Mary, Newington. I had a gelding safe on the 29th of October, in Mr. Harris's stables opposite my house—I saw it safe between ten and eleven o'clock in the forenoon—the prisoner was my servant at the time, and had been so about three weeks—I did not give him authority to sell my horses—I had to go to Hackney, and he went with me to St. George's Church—I told him to go back and clean some bedsteads—I
went home between one and two o'clock in the day, and the horse and the prisoner were gone—I have not seen it since—it was a black pony with three white feet, a white blaze in the forehead, and a white speck on the back.
Prisoner. Did you not desire me to clean the horse and take it to Smithfield? Witness. Yes, but you did not do it.
Prisoner. He desired me, when I could find a customer for it, to sell it. Witness. I never did—I never saw any money for it.
JANE JONES . I am a neighbour of Mr. Wilkinson, and live opposite to him. I might know his little pony if I saw it—I saw the prisoner on the 29th of October, about twelve o'clock, take the pony out—he had the pony coming up the archway.
SIMON SAVORY ( police-constable M 155.) I apprehended the prisoner in High-street, Borough, on the 7th of November—I told him it was on account of stealing a pony of his master—he said he took it to Deptford, and sold it to a person his master knew, but he did not name the person.
Prisoner's Defence. We went to Smithfield and bought another horse—he said, when I got a customer for this other one I might sell it—on Monday morning a man asked if my master had sold the horse—I said no—he said, "If you come to Deptford, I will give you 2 l. 15 s. for it"—they gave me something to drink, and I fell asleep and lost the money—I was not out of the Borough all the time, till I was taken on the 7th of November.
GUILTY .—Aged 18†.— Transported for Ten Years.
[trialAccount t18381126-259]
259. JOHN GILES and JOHN GIBBS were indicted for stealing on the 15th of November, 1100lbs. weight of iron, value 2 l., the goods of Peter Plimley, their master.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN HOGARTH . I am manager of Mr. Plimley's iron-works, in the Borough. Giles was the carman—he had 18 s. a week, and Gibbs was our labourer—we always keep on our premises large quantities of iron—the men have no authority to deal with it without I order them—on the 15th of this month I gave directions to Gibbs to remove some earth at the entrance of the yard at Bankside, to enable the horses to come in better—I sent Giles with the horse and cart to him—I went to the yard again that evening—the horse and cart stood at the entrance of the gate, and I heard them putting some iron hoops which are here into the cart—I put my head over the head of the cart and saw some hoops on the earth which they had just put in—only the two prisoners were in the yard—I asked what the hoops were put in for—they both said "For Ainger and Handy side," a house we do business for—I said that could not be the case, because they had some hoops of us in the afternoon, and sent their own cart for them—the prisoners then seemed confused, and proceeded to take part of the hoops out of the cart again, making some remark that they were only a few hoops—I said, "No, you shall go home with them now in the cart to the Broad wav" where our other yard is—"I will examine them there"—these hoops are broken up to be remelted—they drove the cart to the Broadway—neither of them gave me any account where this iron came from—there was some earth in the cart—there was llcwt. of iron in the cart—the
value of it is about 40 s.—that was the iron that is here—it was under the earth nearly covered, all but the ends of them.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. You told them to drive to the Broadway? A. I did, and I followed them—there was no objection on their part to do it, and they put it on the machine by my order—Giles continued on the premises that Thursday evening—he came on Friday, and then I ordered him to go away—Gibbs was only a labourer—it was not his business to attend to what Giles said—I ordered Giles to go down with the horse and cart, but each of them was ordered by me to put the earth up—there was no iron mixed up with the earth—I saw it out up—I saw them in the act of opening the earth.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Had the iron you found in the cart been lying near the earth? A. No, at the further end of the yard.
JAMES TIDMARSH ( City police-constable No. 51.) I took the prisoners on Friday, and have the iron.
GILES— GUILTY . Aged 23.
GIBBS— GUILTY . Aged 24.
Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18381126-260]
260. JAMES SHUTE was indicted for stealing, on the 30th of October, 1 handkerchief, value 1 s., the goods of George Day, from his person.
GEORGE DAY . I live in Cumberland-street, Hackney-road. About two o'clock on the 30th of October, I was in King-street, Borough—I felt something at my pocket—I turned round and saw the prisoner's jacket stick out, and in it was my handkerchief, which had been in my pocket just before.
Prisoner's Defence. I was going along, another came and put it into my pocket.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 14.— Recommended to mercy.— Confined Five Days, and Whipped.
[trialAccount t18381126-261]
261. ANN FORD was indicted for a misdemeanor.
MESSRS. CHAMBERS and SHEA conducted the Prosecution.
ELIZABETH WISE . My husband, John Wise, keeps an oil-shop in Abbey-street, Bermondsey. At eight o'clock in the morning of the 8th of November, the prisoner came into the shop, and asked for a quarter of a pound of soap, which was 1 1/2 d.—she gave me a crown piece—I gave her 4 s. 10 1/2 d. in change—I put the crown piece into the till, and then took it out and put it into my pocket—I had no other crown in the till nor in my pocket—some time after my husband came in, and asked for some silver—I gave him the crown piece—on Tuesday, the 13th, the prisoner came again about nine o'clock in the morning, for half a pound of candles, which came to 3 1/2 d.—she gave me a bad crown piece—I called my husband and gave it him.
JOHN WIS . I am the husband of the last witness. On the morning of the 8th of November, I asked her for what silver she had, and she gave it me—there was a crown piece in it—on the 13th of November she gave me another crown—I saw it was bad, and gave it to the policeman—I detained the prisoner—I marked the last crown.
Prisoner. You asked your wife if I was the girl, and she said she did not know, and she called her daughter down, and she said she knew me by my bonnet. Witness. My wife had a doubt about her.
ANNA MARIA WIS . I was in the shop on the 8th of November. The prisoner is the woman who came and passed a crown, I am certain—on the 13th my father called me down, and I saw her again.
WILLIAM WEAVE . ( police-constable K 86.) I took the prisoner, and received this crown piece from Mr. Wise—he also gave me this other crown piece—I have had it ever since.
MR. JOHN FIEL . I am Inspector of coin to the Royal Mint. These are both counterfeit, and both from the same mould.
Prisoner's Defence. I met a man in the Borough—he gave me some gin and water, and gave me a crown piece, and told me to get him half a pound of candles, as he was a bricklayer, and going to set a copper—with respect to the other crown, I was not in the place.
GUILTY . Aged 18.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18381126-262]
262. WILLIAM SCOTT was indicted for a misdemeanor.
MESSRS. CHAMBER. and SHE. conducted the Prosecution.
CHARLES BURGBSS GOF . ( police-constable L 81.) On the evening of the 20th of November I was on duty in Tower-street, Blackfriars. I saw the prisoner in company with a young girl—I followed them to Union-street, Borough—I then went up to the prisoner, and took bold of both his hands—I called my brother constable, who took hold of Him by the throat—I saw six bad shillings fall from his mouth—I picked up five, and Brooks took up the other one—I looked at them at the light, and they were all bad.
JAMES BROO . I was with Goff. I followed the prisoner, and throttled him—I saw him trying to swallow something—six bad shillings fell from his mouth—I took up one, and on searching him I found another bad shilling in his pocket, and ten pence farthing of good moneys when the shillings fell on the pavement, I said to Goff, "These dropped from his mouth"—the prisoner said he had none in his mouth—I am quite sure they came from his mouth.
MR. JOHN FIEL . These seven are all counterfeits, and all cast in one mould.
Prisoner's Defence. I was by Blackfriars-road, and saw a piece of dirty rag, and in it were these seven shillings—I put them into my pocket—one fell down the leg of my trowsers—I took them out, and put them into my mouth—I did not know whether they were good or bad.
GUILTY .* Confined One Year.
[trialAccount t18381126-263]
263. GEORGE SANGLIER was indicted for embezzlement.
MESSRS. PHILLIPS and CHAMBERS conducted the Prosecution.
H. W. PARKER, ESQ. I am Assistant Secretary to the Poor Law Commissioners of Lambeth. This is their seal—( producing a paper.)—this document places the parish under a Board of Guardians—I have the confirmation of the prisoner's appointment as master of the workhouse, on the 26th of June last—it is signed by me as their Assistant Secretary.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. This is the constitution drawn up under a particular Act of Parliament? A. Yes, under that Act the guardians of the poor have to appoint the master of the workhouse.
WILLIAM PIGGOTT . I am an army clothier, at No. 12, Old Jewry. For
a considerable time I have sent out shirts to be made by the paupers at Lambeth workhouse—the guardians of the parish are responsible to me for the return of my property—Jackson collects the money for the guardians—on the 26th of July I paid him 6 l. 0 s. 3 d. for Mr. Sanglier the master—I paid him according to these receipts—( looking at them)—on the 14th of August I paid him 3 l. 5 s.— on the 24th of August, 3 l. 5 s., and these are the receipts, from Jackson, as having the money from me.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you pay the money yourself? A. I did—I rather think the receipts were signed before they were brought out—I rather think he gave them to me as they were, without any addition.
GEORGE JACKSON . I am a pauper in Lambeth workhouse. It was part of my business to take home linen, and receive money—I received these three sums, and I paid them over to Sanglier as soon as I went home—it appeared to me that he entered it in a red memorandum book, but I do not know—I paid him other sums besides these—I saw him take a memorandum-book into his hand, and he appeared to write.
Cross-examined. Q. Had you been in the habit of paying a great number of sums to the prisoner? A. Not many—perhaps not once a month at any time—I had received money from Mr. Piggott, three or four times while Sanglier was there—I suppose he was there three months—I have a distinct recollection that enables me to swear positively that I gave the money to him—I paid him the same day that I received them from Mr. Piggott—I cannot exactly tell what I paid, but by the papers, on one is 3 l. 5 s.—I swear I paid that to Mr. Sanglier in gold—Mr. Piggott always paid me with a cheque on his banker's, and I went and got it changed at his banker's—I always paid the prisoner the same day, when I went home—I swear that I paid the three sums on the days here mentioned, in the counting-house—no one was there—it was in the afternoon—I generally went to Mr. Piggott in the morning with the goods—I have been four years in the workhouse.
WILLIAM THOMAS LOGAN . I am clerk of the guardians of Lambeth. Jackson used to receive the money for the work done by the paupers—it was his duty to hand that over to the prisoner, and at the end of the quarter the prisoner was to give me an account of all sums received—he was dismissed on the 16th of October—I saw him on the 18th, and asked him for his account—he rendered me an account of the money he had received, amounting in the whole to 20 l. 18 s. 11 d.—I asked him for the particulars of it—he gave it me—one of his items was "4 l. 4 s. 6 d. received from Mr. Piggott"—I have no doubt of the receipt of it—he did not give me an account of the sum of 6 l. 0 s. 3 d. on the date of this receipt, the 26th of July—he did not give me an account of 3 l. 5 s., nor of 3 l. 5 s.—these three dates are wholly omitted in his account—he has never accounted for these sums before or since—I asked him when he gave his account, whether that was all he had received, he said it was—these receipts are in his hand-writing—it would not be his duty to leave receipts in the hands of Piggott or Jackson, unless he received the money.
Cross-examined. Q. How soon was he to leave? A. The guardians gave him two or three days—I did not perceive that he was in any confusion at having to leave so suddenly—when he gave me his detailed account, he did not say he could not say whether or not it was correct—I swear he said nothing of the kind—there was no charge of this kind made before the Magistrate—no depositions were taken—the Board of Guardians appointed
the prisoner—he was responsible to them—two or three members of the Board are here—the accounts should be audited every three months—they have not been audited the last six months—I had nothing to do with the appointment of the prisoner—he did not say that was the best account he could give, nothing of the kind—he said he was going to live in the neighbourhood, and he would look in, or something to that effect—he did not say for what purpose—the parish came under the dominion of the New Poorlaw in the beginning of the year 1836—the duties of the station filled by the prisoner have increased.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. This charge was not gone into at the police-office? A. No, he was there on another charge—we had not then found out this defalcation—it was Sanglier's duty to account to me, and to me only—I am sure that these several sums were omitted—he had been suspended one week before his actual dismission—he was dismissed on the Tuesday night, and I accounted with him on the Thursday—he was allowed to stay for that week in the workhouse.
WILLIAM PIGGOTT re-examined. I paid the three sums specified—here is a receipt for 4 l. 4 s. 6 d.—I also paid that to Jackson on the 26th of September—that was subsequent to the other three dates—I paid that to Jackson in the same way as the others, and took this receipt.
(George Wilkinson, Esq., of the Northern Circuit, and Recorder of Newcastle; and Thomas Patterson, gave the prisoner a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 34.— Transported for Seven Years.
(There was another indictment against the prisoner and his wife, upon which no evidence was offered.)
[trialAccount t18381126-264]
264. RICHARD FOOT was indicted for stealing, on the 3rd of November, 5lbs. weight of brass, value 5 s.; the goods of John Taylor and others, his masters.
GEORGE SMITH . I am warehousemen to John Taylor and others, brass-founders, in Red Cross-street, Southwark. The prisoner was an errand—boy of theirs—about eleven o'clock, on the 3rd of November, I stopped him going out of the factory, and found ten pieces of brass on him, the property of my employers—he said nothing.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 15.— Recommended to mercy.
Confined Eight Days; Two Days solitary.
ADJOURNED TO MONDAY, DECEMBER 17, 1838.
